She recalled the days she had followed him about his big house with a notebook in her hand, whilst he searched for evidence of his servants’ neglect, drawing his fingers along the polished shelves in the library in a vain search for dust, turning up carpet corners, examining silver, or else counting, as he did regularly every week, the contents of his still-room.

He measured the wine at table and counted the empty bottles, even the corks. It was his boast that in his big garden he could tell the absence of a flower. These he sent to market regularly, with the vegetables he grew and the peaches which ripened on the wall, and woe betide the unlucky gardener who had poached so much as a ripe apple from the orchard, for Harvey had an uncanny instinct which led him to the rifled tree.

She smiled a little wryly at the recollection, and, having completed her change of costume, she went out, locking the door behind her. Her landlady watched her pass down the street, and nodded ominously.

===Chinese===
“好歹他没向你求过爱，亲爱的。”她自言自语，笑了。不知怎么的，她实在想象不出哈维·弗鲁瓦扬向任何人求爱的模样。她想起从前那些日子：她手里拿着记事本，跟着他在那所大宅子里转来转去，而他在搜罗仆役怠慢疏懒的罪证——用手指沿着书房里擦得锃亮的书架一路抹过去，徒劳地找灰；翻起地毯四角；检查银器；再不然就是清点食品配制室里的存货，这桩活计他每周照例要干一遍。

用餐时他要量酒，数空瓶，连软木塞也不放过。他惯常夸口，说自家大花园里少开一朵花，他也能觉察出来。这些花，连同他自种的蔬菜、墙上熟透的桃子，他都定期送往市场出售。哪个倒霉的园丁胆敢偷摘果园里哪怕一只熟苹果，那就等着遭殃吧——哈维有一种说不清道不明的本能，总能一路把他引到那棵被动过手脚的树跟前。

想起这些，她微微苦笑了一下。换装完毕，她便出了门，随手锁上身后的房门。房东太太望着她沿街走远，不祥地点了点头。

===Original===
“Your lodger’s come back,” said a neighbour.

“Yes, she’s come back,” said the woman grimly. “A nice lady she is﻿—I don’t think! It is the first time I’ve ever had a crook in my house, and it’ll be the last. I am giving her notice tonight.”

Unconscious of the criticism, Thalia boarded a bus which took her into the city. She got down in Fleet Street, went into the large office of a popular newspaper. At the desk she took an advertisement form, looked at the white sheet for a moment thoughtfully, then wrote:

===Chinese===
“你家房客回来啦。”一位邻居说。

“是啊，回来了。”女人咬牙切齿地说，“好一位‘体面小姐’——我看才不是呢！这号骗子头一回住进我家，也是最后一回。今晚我就给她下逐客令。”

塔莉娅全然不知自己正被人这样议论，上了一辆公共汽车进了城。她在舰队街（Fleet Street）下车，走进一家热门报纸宽大的营业部。在受理台前，她取了一张广告登记单，对着那张白纸若有所思地端详了片刻，然后写道：

===Original===
**Secretary.**﻿—Young lady from the Colonies requires post as Secretary. Resident-Secretary preferred. Small wages required. Shorthand and Typewriting.

She left a space for the box number, handed the advertisement across the counter, and paid the fee.

She was back again in Lexington Street in time for tea, a meal which was brought up to her on a battered tray by her landlady.

===Chinese===
**秘书。**——殖民地来的年轻女士谋求秘书职位，愿任住家秘书，薪资要求从廉。通速记与打字。

她留出待填信箱号的空白，把广告单隔着柜台递了过去，付了费用。

她赶回莱克星顿街，正赶上喝下午茶——这顿茶点，是房东太太用一只破旧的托盘给她端上楼来的。

===Original===
“Look here, Miss Drummond,” said that worthy person, “I’ve got a few words to say to you.”

“Say them,” said the girl carelessly.

“I shall want your room after next week.”

===Chinese===
“你听着，德拉蒙德小姐，”这位可敬的人物开口道，“我有几句话要对你讲。”

“那就讲吧。”姑娘满不在乎地说。

“过了下个星期，我就要用你那间房了。”

===Original===
Thalia turned slowly.

“Does that mean I’ve got to get out?”

“That’s what it means. I can’t have people like you staying in a respectable house. I’m surprised at you, a young lady as I always thought you were.”

“Continue to think so,” said Thalia coolly. “I’m both young and ladylike.”

But the stout landlady was not to be checked in her well-rehearsed indictment.

===Chinese===
塔莉娅慢慢转过身来。

“这是说，我得搬出去了？”

“就是这个意思。我这么一户体面人家，容不得你这种人住下去。你真让我意外——我一直把你看成一位正经小姐来着。”

“那就接着这么想吧。”塔莉娅冷冷地说，“我又年轻，又是淑女。”

然而，谁也拦不住这位胖房东太太那篇演练娴熟的控诉词。

===Original===
“A nice lady you are,” she said, “giving my house a bad name. You’ve been in prison for a week. Perhaps you don’t think I know, but I read the newspapers.”

“I’m sure you do,” said the girl quietly. “That will do, Mrs. Boled. I leave your house next week.”

“And I should like to say﻿—” began the woman.

“Say it on the mat,” said Thalia, and closed the door in the choleric lady’s face.

===Chinese===
“好一位正经小姐，”她说，“把我这房子的名声都糟蹋了。你蹲了一个星期的班房。兴许你以为我不知道，可我是看报的。”

“这我相信。”姑娘平静地说，“够了，博莱德太太（Mrs. Boled）。下星期我就搬出你这儿。”

“我还想说——”女人刚起了个头。

“想说就冲着门垫说去吧。”塔莉娅说罢，当着这位怒气冲冲的太太的面把门关上了。

===Original===
As it was now growing dark, she lit a kerosene lamp and occupied the evening by manicuring her nails, an operation which was interrupted by the arrival of the nine o’clock post. She heard the rat-tat at the door and the heavy feet of her landlady on the stairs.

“A letter for you,” called the woman.

Thalia unlocked the door and took the envelope from the landlady’s hand.

===Chinese===
天色渐黑，她点起一盏煤油灯，靠修剪指甲来消磨这个晚上。这桩活计做到一半，被九点钟的邮班打断了——她听见笃笃的叩门声，还有房东太太上楼的沉重脚步。

“有你的信。”女人喊道。

塔莉娅开了锁，从房东太太手里接过信封。

===Original===
“You had better tell your friends that you’re going to get a new address,” said the woman, loath to leave her quarrel half-finished.

“I haven’t told my friends yet that I live in such a horrible place,” said Thalia sweetly, and locked the door before the woman could think of a suitable reply.

She smiled as she carried the envelope to the light. It was addressed in printed characters. She turned it over, looking at the postmark before she opened it, and extracted a thick white card. At the first glance of the message her face changed its expression.

===Chinese===
“你最好告诉你那些朋友，你就要换新住址了。”女人说——这场架没吵完，她实在不甘心就此撒手。

“我还没告诉朋友们，我住在这么个糟糕透顶的地方呢。”塔莉娅甜甜地说，没等女人想出一句合适的回敬，就锁上了房门。

她把信封拿到灯前，唇边带着微笑。信封上的地址是印刷体。她把信封翻过来，先看了看邮戳才拆开，从中抽出一张厚实的白色卡片。那行字才扫进眼里，她的脸色就变了。

===Original===
The card was a square one, and in the centre was a large crimson circle. Within the circle was written in the same printed characters:

We have need of you. Enter the car which you will find waiting at the corner of Steyne Square at ten o’clock tomorrow night.

She put the card down on the table and stared at it.

The Crimson Circle had need of her!

She had expected the summons, but it had come earlier than she had anticipated.

===Chinese===
那是一张方形卡片，正中印着一个大大的深红色圆圈。圆圈里用同样的印刷体写着：

我们用得着你。明天夜里十点，请乘上在斯泰恩广场（Steyne Square）拐角处等候你的汽车。

她把卡片搁在桌上，怔怔地盯着它。

红圈竟用得着她！

这道召唤她本已料到，只是它来得比她预想的更早。

## XI / 十一

===Original===

The Confession

===Chinese===

供词

===Original===

At three minutes to ten the following night, a closed car drove slowly into Steyne Square and came to a halt at the corner of Clarges Street. A few minutes later Thalia Drummond walked into the square from the other end. She wore a long black cloak, and the little hat upon her head was held in its position by a thick veil knotted under her chin.

Without a second’s hesitation she opened the door of the car and stepped in. It was in complete darkness, but she could see the figure of the driver indistinctly. He did not turn his head, nor did he attempt to start the car, although she felt the vibration of the engines beneath her feet.

“You were charged at the Marylebone Police Court yesterday morning with theft,” said the driver without preamble. “Yesterday afternoon you inserted an advertisement, describing yourself as a newly-arrived colonial, your intention being to find another situation, where you could continue your career of petty pilfering.”

===Chinese===

次日夜里，差三分十点，一辆带篷轿车缓缓驶入斯泰恩广场，在克拉杰斯街（Clarges Street）拐角处停了下来。几分钟后，塔莉娅·德拉蒙德从广场另一头走了进来。她身披一件黑色长斗篷，头上那顶小帽，是用一幅在颏下打结的厚面纱扣住的。

她没有片刻迟疑，拉开车门坐了进去。车内一片漆黑，但她仍能隐约辨出司机的身影。他没有回头，也没有发车的意思，尽管她脚下感觉得到发动机的震颤。

“昨天上午，你在玛丽勒本警务法庭（Marylebone Police Court）被控偷窃。”司机开门见山地说，“昨天下午，你登了一则广告，自称新近从殖民地来，打算另谋一份差事，好接着干你那小偷小摸的营生。”

===Original===

“This is very interesting,” said Thalia without a tremor of voice, “but you did not bring me here to give me my past history. When I had your letter I guessed that you thought I would be a very useful assistant. But there is one question I want to ask you.”

“If I wish to reply I shall,” was the uncompromising answer.

“I realise that,” said Thalia, with a faint smile in the darkness. “Suppose I had communicated with the police and I had come here attended by Mr. Parr and the clever Mr. Derrick Yale?”

“You would have been lying on the pavement dead by now,” was the calm announcement. “Miss Drummond, I am going to put easy money in your way and find you a very excellent job. I do not even mind if you indulge in your eccentricity in your spare time, but your principal task will be to serve me. You understand?”

===Chinese===

“这可真有意思。”塔莉娅声色不动地说，“不过你把我带到这儿来，总不是为了给我数说老底。收到你的信时我就猜到，你准是觉得我这个人当帮手大有用处。只是有一个问题，我想问你。”

“我愿意答的时候，自然会答。”回答毫不通融。

“这我明白。”塔莉娅说，黑暗中浮起一丝浅笑，“倘或我早已知会了警方，由帕尔先生和那位精明的德里克·耶尔先生陪着，一道到这儿来呢？”

“那你这会儿早就直挺挺躺在人行道上了。”对方的宣告不慌不忙，“德拉蒙德小姐，我要给你指一条轻易来钱的路，替你找一份顶好的差事。闲暇时候你尽可以由着自己的怪癖行事，我不计较，但你的头等要务，是为我效力。明白吗？”

===Original===

She nodded, and then realising he could not see her, she said:

“Yes.”

“You will be well paid for everything you do; I shall always be on hand to help you﻿—or to punish you if you attempt to betray me,” he added. “Do you understand?”

“Perfectly,” she replied.

===Chinese===

她点了点头，随即想到他看不见自己，便开口说道：

“明白。”

“你办的每一件事，都会有丰厚的报酬；我会随时在旁照应你——你若企图出卖我，也随时惩罚你。”他又添了一句，“明白吗？”

“再明白不过了。”她答道。

===Original===

“Your job will be a very simple one,” went on the unknown driver. “You will present yourself at Brabazon’s Bank tomorrow. Brabazon is in need of a secretary.”

“But will he employ me?” she interrupted. “Must I go in another name?”

“Go in your own name,” said the man impatiently. “Don’t interrupt. I will pay you two hundred pounds for your services. Here is the money.” He thrust two notes over his shoulder and she took them.

Her hand accidentally touched his shoulder, and she felt something hard beneath his fleecy coat.

===Chinese===

“你要干的活儿非常简单。”那位身份不明的司机接着说，“明天你就上布拉巴宗银行（Brabazon’s Bank）报到去。布拉巴宗正缺一位秘书。”

“可他会雇我吗？”她插话道，“我得换个名字去吗？”

“就用你自己的名字去。”那人不耐烦地说，“别打岔。我付你两百镑酬金。钱在这儿。”他从肩头往后递过两张钞票，她伸手接了。

她的手无意间碰到他的肩膀，隔着那件绒面外套，摸到一处硬邦邦的东西。

===Original===

“A bulletproof waistcoat,” she noted mentally, and then aloud: “What am I to say to Mr. Brabazon about my earlier experience?”

“It will be unnecessary to say anything, or do anything. You will receive your instructions from time to time. That is all,” he added shortly.

A few minutes later Thalia Drummond sat in the corner of the taxicab which was taking her back to Lexington Street. Behind her, at intervals, came another taxicab which slowed when hers did, but never overtook her, not even when she descended at the corner of the street where her lodgings were situated. And when she turned the key of her street door, Inspector Parr was only a dozen paces from her. If she knew that she was being shadowed, she made no sign.

===Chinese===

“防弹背心。”她心里暗暗记下这一笔，随即出声问道，“关于我先前那段经历，我该对布拉巴宗先生怎么说？”

“什么也不必说，什么也不必做。你会随时接到给你的指令。就这样。”他简短地补了一句。

几分钟后，塔莉娅·德拉蒙德已坐在一辆送她回莱克星顿街的出租汽车角落里。她身后隔着一段距离还跟着一辆出租汽车，她的车一慢它也慢，却始终不超上来，连她在寓所所在街口下车时也不例外。待她掏出钥匙去开临街大门时，帕尔探长离她不过十几步远。她若知道自己被人盯了梢，至少没有露出半点迹象。

===Original===

Parr only waited for a few minutes, watching the house from the opposite side of the roadway, and then, as her light appeared in the upper window, he turned and walked thoughtfully back to the cab which had brought him so far eastward.

He had opened the door of the cab and was stepping in, when somebody passed him on the sidewalk; somebody who was walking briskly with his collar turned up, but Inspector Parr knew him.

“Flush,” he called sharply, and the man turned round on his heel.

He was a little dark, thin-faced, lithe man, at the sight of the Inspector his jaw dropped.

===Chinese===

帕尔只等了几分钟，站在马路对面盯着那栋房子，直到楼上窗口亮起她的灯光，才转身若有所思地走回那辆载他老远跑到城东来的出租汽车。

他拉开车门，正要跨进去，忽有一个人在人行道上擦身而过；那人竖着衣领、步履匆匆，可帕尔探长认得他。

“弗拉什（Flush）。”他厉声一唤，那人应声转过身来。

他是个矮小黝黑、尖脸瘦身、动作轻捷的汉子，一看见探长，下巴顿时耷拉了下来。

===Original===

“Why﻿—why, Mr. Parr,” he said, with ill-affected geniality, “whoever thought of seeing you in this part of the world?”

“I want a little talk with you, Flush. Will you walk along with me?”

It was an ominous invitation, which Mr. “Flush” had heard before.

“You haven’t got anything against me, Mr. Parr?” he said loudly.

===Chinese===

“哎呀——哎呀，帕尔先生。”他说着，强装出一副亲热劲儿，却怎么也装不像，“谁能想到会在这个地界撞见您哪？”

“我想跟你聊几句，弗拉什。陪我走一段怎么样？”

这是个不祥的邀请，“弗拉什”先生从前可是听过的。

“您该没有抓着我什么把柄吧，帕尔先生？”他大声说。

===Original===

“Nothing,” admitted Parr. “Besides, you’re going straight now. I seem to remember you telling me that the day you came out of prison.”

“That’s right,” said “Flush” Barnet, heaving a sigh of relief. “Going straight, working for my living, and engaged to be married.”

“You don’t tell me?” said the stout Mr. Parr with well-simulated astonishment. “And is it Bella or Milly?”

“It is Milly,” said “Flush,” inwardly cursing the excellent memory of the police inspector. “She’s going straight, too. She’s got a job at one of the shops.”

===Chinese===

“没有。”帕尔承认道，“再说，你如今也改邪归正了。我记得你出狱那天，好像就是这么跟我说的。”

“可不是嘛。”“弗拉什”·巴尼特说着，如释重负地舒了一口气，“改邪归正，自食其力，还订了亲，眼看就要成家。”

“竟有这等事？”发福的帕尔先生装出一副大为吃惊的样子说，“那么，是贝拉（Bella）呢，还是米莉（Milly）？”

“是米莉。”“弗拉什”说，心里暗暗咒骂这位探长过人的记性，“她也改邪归正了，在一家商铺找到了活儿。”

===Original===

“At Brabazon’s Bank, to be exact,” said the inspector, and then turned as though some thought had arrested him. “I wonder,” he muttered, “I wonder if that is it?”

“She’s a perfect young lady, is Milly,” Mr. “Flush” hastened to explain. “Honest as the day, wouldn’t swipe a clock, not if her life depended on it. I don’t want you to think she is bad, Mr. Parr, because she’s not. We’re both living what I might term an honest life.”

Parr’s placid face wrinkled in a smile.

“That’s grand news you’re telling me, ‘Flush.’ Where is Milly to be found in these days?”

===Chinese===

“说得确切些，是在布拉巴宗银行。”探长说罢便扭过身去，仿佛被什么念头拽住了。“怪了，”他喃喃自语，“怪了，莫非就是这么回事？”

“米莉是位规规矩矩的小姐。”“弗拉什”先生忙不迭地解释，“为人诚实透顶，就是拿性命逼她，她也决不肯顺手牵羊一只钟。我可不想要您把她想坏了，帕尔先生，她可不是那种人。我们俩如今过的，用我的话讲，都算是安分日子。”

帕尔那张平和的脸泛起了笑纹。

“你给我带来的是天大的好消息啊，弗拉什。这些日子，要上哪儿去找米莉？”

===Original===

“She’s living in diggings on the other side of the river,” said “Flush” reluctantly. “You’re not going to rake up old scandals, are you, Mr. Parr?”

“Heaven forbid,” said Inspector Parr piously. “No, I’d like to have a talk with her. Perhaps﻿—” he hesitated, “anyway, it can wait. It was rather providential meeting you, ‘Flush.’ ”

But “Flush” did not share that view, even though he expressed a faint acquiescence.

“So that’s it,” said Inspector Parr to himself, but he did not express the nature of his suspicions, even when he met Derrick Yale at his club half-an-hour later. And it was a further curious fact, that though they touched every aspect of the Crimson Circle mystery in the long conversation which followed, never once did Mr. Parr mention Thalia Drummond’s interview, which, if he had not seen, he had at least guessed.

===Chinese===

“她住在河对岸的寄宿公寓里。”“弗拉什”不情愿地说，“您总不至于要翻人家的旧账吧，帕尔先生？”

“上天不容。”帕尔探长一脸虔敬地说，“不，我不过想同她谈一谈。也许——”他迟疑了一下，“反正这事不急。遇上你，可真是天意啊，弗拉什。”

可惜“弗拉什”并不作此想，尽管他口头上也淡淡地表示了赞同。

“原来根子在这儿。”帕尔探长心里暗想。但他没有把这层怀疑说破，连半小时后在俱乐部遇上德里克·耶尔时也没提。还有一桩更蹊跷的事：在随后那场长谈里，两人把红圈疑案的方方面面都碰了个遍，帕尔先生却始终只字不提塔莉娅·德拉蒙德的那场会面——那场面他纵然没有亲眼看见，至少也猜了个八九不离十。

===Original===

The two men left early the next morning for the little country town where one Ambrose Sibly, described as an able-seaman, was held on a charge of murder. At his own earnest request, Jack Beardmore was allowed to accompany them, though he was not present at the interview between the two detectives and the sullen man who had slain his father.

A brawny, unshaven fellow, half Scottish, half Swede, Sibly proved to be. He could neither read nor write, and had been in the hands of the police before. This much Parr had discovered from a reference of his fingerprints.

At first he was not inclined to commit himself, and it was rather Derrick Yale’s skilful cross-examination, than Inspector Parr’s efforts, which produced the confession.

===Chinese===

次日一早，两人动身前往那座乡间小镇——一个名叫安布罗斯·西布利（Ambrose Sibly）、登记职业是熟练水手的人，正因一桩杀人指控被关押在那里。经杰克·比德莫尔再三恳求，警方允许他同行，只是在两位侦探提审那个阴沉着脸、杀害他父亲的汉子时，他没有在场。

西布利原来是个膀阔腰圆、满脸胡茬的汉子，一半苏格兰血统，一半瑞典血统。他目不识丁，从前也落到过警察手里。这些底细，帕尔是从他的指纹档案里查出来的。

起初他并不肯松口，最后问出这份供词的，与其说是帕尔探长的努力，不如说是德里克·耶尔那手腕高明的盘问。

===Original===

“Yes, I did it all right,” he said at last.

They were seated in the cell with an official shorthand-writer taking a note of his statement.

“You’ve got me proper, but you wouldn’t have got me if I hadn’t been drunk. And whilst I’m confessing, I might as well own up that I killed Harry Hobbs. He was a shipmate of mine on the *Oritianga* in 1912﻿—they can only hang me once. Killed him and chucked his body overboard, I did, over the question of a woman that we met at Newport News, which is in America. I’ll tell you how this happened, gentlemen. I lost my ship about a month ago, and was stranded at the Sailors’ Home at Wapping. I got chucked out of there for being drunk, and on top of that I was locked up and got seven days’ imprisonment. If the old fool had only given me a month I shouldn’t have been here. One night after I came out of prison I was walking through the East End, down on my luck and starving for a drink, and feeling properly miserable. To make it worse, I had the toothache﻿—”

===Chinese===

“对，人是我杀的，没错。”他终于开了口。

他们坐在牢房里，一名官方速记员把他的供述一字一句记录在案。

“你们算是把我办得结结实实，可我要不是喝醉了，你们也拿不着我。反正我都在招了，索性连哈里·霍布斯（Harry Hobbs）也一并招了吧——1912 年他是我在‘奥里蒂安加’号（Oritianga）上的船友——反正绞索只能套我一回。我杀了他，还把尸首丢下了海，起因是我们在纽波特纽斯（Newport News）搭上的一个女人——那地方在美国。这档子事是怎么来的，二位先生听我说。约莫一个月前，我丢了船上的差事，流落在瓦平（Wapping）的海员之家。因为酗酒，我被那儿撵了出来，外加吃了官司，蹲了七天班房。那老糊涂当初要是判我一个月，我今天就不会蹲在这儿了。出狱后的一天夜里，我正走在伦敦东区，倒霉透顶，馋酒馋得要命，心里憋屈得没个说处。更糟的是，偏偏又牙疼上了——”

===Original===

Parr met Derrick Yale’s eyes, and Derrick smiled faintly.

“I was loafing along the edge of the pavement looking for cigarette ends, and thinking of nothing except where I could get a bit of food and a night’s lodging. It was beginning to rain, too, and it looked as though I was going to have another night on the streets, when I heard a voice say, almost in my ear, ‘Jump in.’ I looked round. A motorcar was standing by the side of the roadway. I couldn’t believe my ears. Presently the man in the car said ‘Jump in. It’s you I mean!’ and he mentioned my name. We drove along for a while without his saying anything, and I noticed that he kept clear of all the streets where the big lights were.

“After a bit he stopped the car, and began to tell me who I was. I can assure you I was surprised. He knew the whole of my history. He even knew about Harry Hobbs﻿—I was tried for that killing and acquitted﻿—and then he asked me if I’d like to earn a hundred pounds. I told him I would, and he said there was an old gentleman in the country who had done him a lot of harm, and he wanted him ‘outed.’ I didn’t want to take the job on for some time, but he gave me such a lot of talk about how he could get me hung for Hobbs’s murder, and how it was safe, and he’d give me a bicycle to get away on, and at last I agreed.

===Chinese===

帕尔与德里克·耶尔目光一碰，德里克淡淡一笑。

“我顺着马路边上溜达着捡烟头，脑子里什么念头都没有，光想着上哪儿弄一口吃的、寻一夜的宿头。天又下起雨来，眼瞧着又得在大街上过一夜了，就在这时，我听见一个声音，几乎贴着我耳朵说：‘上车。’我四下一看，一辆汽车停在路旁。我简直不敢相信自己的耳朵。过了一会儿，车里那人说：‘上车，叫的就是你！’还报出了我的名字。车往前开了一程，他一言不发，我留意到他始终避着那些灯火通明的大街不走。

“过了一阵，他停下车，开始把我的来历一五一十抖落出来。我敢说，我当时惊得非同小可。他把我这辈子的事摸了个通透。连哈里·霍布斯那桩他也知道——我为那桩命案受过审，后来无罪开释——随后他问我，想不想挣一百镑。我说想。他就说，乡间有位老先生坑害他不浅，他要这人‘归西’。我好一阵子不想接这桩活儿，可他左一套右一套，说他有法子叫我因霍布斯的命案被绞死，又说这事如何稳当，还给我备了一辆自行车好脱身，末了我应承了下来。

===Original===

“He picked me up by arrangement a week later in Steyne Square. Then he gave me all the final particulars. I got down to Beardmore’s place soon after it was dark, and hid in the wood. He told me Mr. Beardmore generally walked through the wood every morning, and that I was to make myself comfortable for the night. I hadn’t been in the wood an hour when I had a fright. I heard somebody moving. I think it must have been a gamekeeper. He was a big fellow, and I only just got a glimpse of him.

“And I think that’s about all, gentlemen, except that the next morning the old fellow came in the wood and I shot him. I don’t remember much about it, for I was drunk at the time, having taken a bottle of whisky into the wood with me. But I was sober enough to get on to the bicycle, and I rode off. And I should have got away altogether, if it hadn’t been for the booze.”

“And that is all?” asked Parr, when the confession had been read over and the man had affixed a rough cross.

===Chinese===

“照事先约好，一星期后他在斯泰恩广场接了我，把最后的细节一一交代清楚。天擦黑不久，我便到了比德莫尔的宅子，躲进树林。他告诉我，比德莫尔先生每天清晨照例要从树林里穿行一趟，叫我安心歇一夜就是。我进树林还不到一个钟头，就受了一场惊吓。我听见有人走动，想来准是个猎场看守。那是个大块头，我只勉强瞥见他一眼。

“我想大概就这些了，二位。只是第二天早晨，那老头进了树林，我一枪结果了他。这事我记得的不多，因为当时我醉着——进树林时我随身带了一瓶威士忌。不过跨上自行车逃走这点神志，我倒还有，一路骑走了。要不是贪那口黄汤，我本来是能整个儿脱身的。”

“就这些了？”供词念过一遍、那人画了个歪歪扭扭的十字为记后，帕尔问道。

===Original===

“That’s all, guv’nor,” said the sailor.

“And you don’t know who it was who employed you?”

“Not the faintest idea,” said the other cheerfully. “There’s one thing about him, though, I could tell you,” he said after a pause. “He kept using a word that I’ve never heard before. I’m not highly educated, but I’ve noticed that some men have favourite words. We had an old skipper who always used the word ‘morbid.’ ”

“What was the word?” asked Parr.

===Chinese===

“就这些，长官。”水手说。

“那么，雇你办事的那个人是谁，你也不知道？”

“半点影子都没有。”对方倒是答得乐呵呵，“不过关于他，有件事我倒可以说给您听。”他顿了顿说，“他老是翻来覆去用一个词儿，我从没听人用过。我没念过什么书，可我留意过，有的男人说话偏爱使某个词。我们船上从前有个老船长，开口闭口总挂着‘病态’这个词。”

“是个什么词？”帕尔问。

===Original===

The man scratched his head.

“I’ll remember it and let you know,” he said, and they left him to his meditations, which were few, and probably not unpleasant.

Four hours after, the jailor took Ambrose Sibly some food. He was lying on his bed, and the jailor shook him by the shoulder.

===Chinese===

那人挠了挠头。

“我想起来了会告诉你们的。”他说。两人便任他自己琢磨去——他那点心事少得很，多半也不至于不痛快。

四个钟头之后，狱卒给安布罗斯·西布利送饭来。他正躺在床上，狱卒摇了摇他的肩膀。

===Original===

“Wake up,” he said, but Ambrose Sibly never woke again.

He was stone dead.

And in the tin dipper, half-filled with water, which stood by his bed, and with which he had slaked his thirst, they found sufficient hydrocyanic acid to kill fifty men.

But it was not the poison which interested Inspector Parr so much as the little circle of crimson paper which was found floating on the top of the water.

===Chinese===

“醒醒。”他说。可安布罗斯·西布利再也醒不过来了。

他已经死透了。

他床边立着一只盛了半勺水的锡皮水勺——他方才就是用它解的渴——人们在里面发现的氢氰酸，足足毒得死五十个人。

然而，比起那毒药来，更让帕尔探长上心的，是浮在水面上的一小片深红色纸圈。

## XII / 十二

===Original===

The Pointed Boots

===Chinese===

尖头靴

===Original===

Mr. Felix Marl sat behind the locked door of his bedroom, and he was engaged in a task which had the elements of unpleasant familiarity.

Twenty-five years before, when he was an inmate of the big French prison at Toulouse, he had worked in a bootmaker’s shop, and the handling of boots was an everyday experience. It is true his business had been to repair, and not to destroy. Today, with a razor-sharp knife, he was cutting to shreds a pair of pointed patent leather shoes which he had only worn three times. Strip by strip he cut the leather, which he then placed on the fire.

Some men live intensely and suffer intensely. Mr. Felix Marl was one of those who could crowd into a day the terrors of an aeon. In some manner a newspaper had got hold of the story of the footprint in Beardmore’s ground, and a new fear had been added to the many which confused and paralysed this big man. He was sitting in his shirt sleeves, the perspiration rolling down his face, for the fire was a big one and the room was super-heated.

===Chinese===

菲利克斯·马尔先生坐在自己卧室反锁的房门后面，正埋头干着一桩活计——这活计他干来，竟带着几分令人不快的熟稔。

二十五年前，他蹲法国图卢兹（Toulouse）大监狱的时候，曾在狱中的靴匠铺里做活，摆弄靴子是每天的家常便饭。不假，当年他的营生是修补，不是销毁。今天，他手里一把剃刀般锋利的刀子，正把一双只上过三回脚的尖头漆皮鞋裁成碎片。皮子一条一条割下来，他便随手搁进火里。

有的人活得炽烈，也苦得炽烈。菲利克斯·马尔先生便是那一路人，能把万古的恐怖塞进一日之间尽数熬过。不知怎的，比德莫尔家宅园里那个脚印的事竟叫一家报纸抓到了，于是新的恐惧又添进了那团早已搅得这大个子又乱又瘫的恐惧里。他只穿着衬衫坐在那儿，汗珠子顺着脸往下淌——炉火烧得极旺，屋里热得过了头。

===Original===

Presently the last shred was thrown into the fire and he sat watching it grill and flame before he put away the knife, washed his hands and opened the windows to let out the acrid odour of burning leather.

It would have been better, he thought, if he had carried out his first resolution, and he cursed himself for the cowardice which had induced him to substitute his revolver for a fountain pen. But he was safe. Nobody had seen him leave the grounds.

With such men as he, blind panic and unreasoning confidence succeed one another, almost as a natural reaction. By the time he had descended his stairs to his little library he had almost forgotten that he was in any danger.

===Chinese===

不多时，最后一条碎皮也丢进了火里；他坐在那儿，望着它在火中烤卷、蹿起火舌，然后才收起刀子，洗净双手，推开窗户，放出烧皮革那股刺鼻的焦臭。

他想，当初若是照头一个决断办成了，如今倒省事；他咒骂自己那股怂劲——正是那股怂劲，让他放下了左轮手枪，改用一支自来水笔。可他终究是安全的。没有人瞧见他离开那片宅园。

像他这路人，盲目的惊惶与无来由的自信你消我长，几乎像一种天生的反作用。等他下了楼、走进那间小书房，自己正身处险境这回事，他几乎已经忘光了。

===Original===

In the fading light of day he had written a conciliatory, even a grovelling letter, and had, as he believed, delivered it safely. Would it be found? He had another moment of panic.

“Pshaw!” said Mr. Marl, and dismissed that dangerous possibility.

His servant brought him a tea-tray and arranged it on a small table by the side of his desk, where the big man sat.

===Chinese===

暮色渐沉时分，他写了一封赔罪示好、甚至低三下四的信，并且自以为已稳稳当当送到了地方。那封信会被翻出来么？他心口又是一阵发慌。

“呸！”马尔先生啐了一声，把这层凶险的可能从脑子里撵了出去。

仆人给他端来茶盘，摆在书桌旁边的一张小桌上；那大个子就在书桌前坐着。

===Original===

“Will you see that gentleman now, sir?”

“Eh?” said Mr. Marl, turning round. “Which gentleman?”

“I told you there was a man who wanted to see you.”

Marl remembered that his boot-destroying operation had been interrupted by a knock.

===Chinese===

“先生，现在见那位先生吗？”

“嗯？”马尔先生回过头来，“哪位先生？”

“我回过您了，有个人要见您。”

马尔这才记起，方才那场毁靴的活计，正是叫一阵敲门声打断的。

===Original===

“Who is he?” he asked.

“I put his card on the table, sir.”

“Didn’t you tell him that I was engaged?”

“Yes, but he said he’d wait until you came down.”

===Chinese===

“他是谁？”他问。

“名片我搁在桌上了，先生。”

“你没告诉他我正有事脱不开身？”

“说了，可他说他愿意等，一直等到您下楼。”

===Original===

The man handed him the card, and Mr. Marl reading it, jumped and turned a sickly yellow.

“Inspector Parr,” he said unsteadily. “What does he want with me?”

His shaking hand fingered his mouth.

“Show him in,” he said with an effort.

===Chinese===

仆人把名片递过去。马尔先生一看，整个人跳了起来，脸色转成一片病态的蜡黄。

“帕尔探长，”他声音不稳地说，“他找我干什么？”

他那只发抖的手在嘴上摩挲。

“请他进来。”他费了把劲才吐出这一句。

===Original===

He had not met Inspector Parr either professionally or socially, and his first glance at the little man reassured him. There was nothing particularly menacing in the appearance of the red-faced detective.

“Sit down, inspector. I’m sorry I was busy when you came,” said Mr. Marl. When he was agitated his voice was almost birdlike in its thinness.

Parr sat down on the edge of the nearest chair, balancing his Derby hat on his knee.

“I thought I’d wait until you came down, Mr. Marl. I wanted to see you about this Beardmore murder.”

Mr. Marl said nothing. With an effort he kept his trembling lips from quivering, and assumed, as he believed, an air of polite interest.

===Chinese===

帕尔探长这人，他无论公务应酬还是私下交际都不曾会过，而这小个子一照面，倒叫他放了心。这位红脸膛侦探的仪表里，瞧不出半点特别的煞气。

“请坐，探长。您来的时候我正忙着，失礼了。”马尔先生说。他一激动，嗓音便细得几乎像鸟啼。

帕尔在最近那把椅子的边沿上落了座，圆顶硬礼帽端平稳稳架在膝头。

“我想着索性等您下楼，马尔先生。我要见您，是为比德莫尔凶杀案的事。”

马尔先生没吭声。他强自按住颤抖的嘴唇不让它哆嗦，又摆出一副——他自己认定是——客气而专注的神气。

===Original===

“You knew Mr. Beardmore very well?”

“Not very well,” said Marl. “I certainly have had business dealings with him.”

“Have you met him before?”

Marl hesitated. He was the kind of man to whom a lie came most readily, and his natural habit of mind was to state the exact opposite of the truth.

===Chinese===

“您同比德莫尔先生很熟？”

“说不上很熟，”马尔说，“不过同他确有过生意上的往来。”

“从前见过他没有？”

马尔迟疑起来。他是那种谎话最容易出口的人，心思的天性便是把实情恰恰倒过来讲。

===Original===

“No,” he admitted. “I had seen him years ago, but that was before he had grown a beard.”

“Where was Mr. Beardmore when you were coming into the house?” asked Parr.

“He was standing on the terrace,” replied Marl with unnecessary loudness.

===Chinese===

“没有，”他招认道，“多年以前倒是见过一回，不过那阵子他还没蓄胡子。”

“您进宅子的时候，比德莫尔先生人在哪儿？”帕尔问。

“他站在露台上。”马尔回答，嗓门大得没有必要。

===Original===

“And you saw him?”

Marl nodded.

“They tell me, Mr. Marl,” Parr went on, looking down at his hat, “that for some reason or other you were startled﻿—Mr. Jack Beardmore says that he thought you were momentarily terrified. What was the cause of that?”

Mr. Marl shrugged his shoulders and forced a smile.

“I think I explained it was a little heart attack. I am subject to them,” he said.

===Chinese===

“您看见他了？”

马尔点了点头。

“人家告诉我，马尔先生，”帕尔接着往下问，眼睛望着自己的帽子，“说您不知怎么着吃了一惊——杰克·比德莫尔先生说，他看您当时吓得不轻。这是何故？”

马尔先生耸了耸肩，硬挤出一个笑容。

“我记得我解释过了，那是心脏病小发作。我这毛病是常犯的。”他说。

===Original===

Parr had turned his hat so that he was looking into the interior, and he did not raise his eyes when he asked:

“It was not the sight of Mr. Beardmore?”

“Of course not,” said the other vigorously. “Why should I be scared of Mr. Beardmore? I’ve had a lot of correspondence with him, and know him almost as well﻿—”

===Chinese===

帕尔把帽子转了个面，正对着帽膛里瞅着，问话时眼皮也不抬：

“不是瞧见了比德莫尔先生才吓着的？”

“当然不是，”对方答得理直气壮，“我凭什么怕比德莫尔先生？我同他书信往来多得是，熟得差不多跟——”

===Original===

“But you hadn’t met him for years?”

“I hadn’t seen him for years,” corrected Marl irritably.

“And the cause of your agitation was just a heart attack, Mr. Marl?” asked the inspector.

For the first time his eyes rose and fixed themselves upon the other’s.

“Absolutely.” Marl’s voice did not lack heartiness. “I had forgotten all about my little seizure until you reminded me.”

===Chinese===

“可你们不是多年没见过面了吗？”

“我是多年没见过他。”马尔没好气地纠正道。

“那么您那场慌乱，不过是心脏病发作而已，马尔先生？”探长问。

他这才头一回抬起眼来，定定地落在对方的眼睛上。

“千真万确。”马尔的口气不缺热络劲儿。“您不提，我早把那场小发作忘得精光了。”

===Original===

“There is another point I wanted cleared up,” said the detective. His attention had gone back to his fascinating hat, which he was turning over and over mechanically until it had the appearance of a revolving butter-churn. “When you came to Mr. Beardmore’s house you were wearing pointed patent shoes.”

Marl frowned.

“Was I? I’ve forgotten.”

===Chinese===

“还有一桩想请您澄清。”侦探说。他的注意力又回到那顶叫他心醉神迷的帽子上，机械地翻过来转过去，直到那帽子活脱脱成了一架团团打转的黄油搅桶。“您上比德莫尔先生府上那天，脚上穿的是尖头漆皮鞋。”

马尔皱起了眉头。

“是吗？我忘了。”

===Original===

“Did you take any walk into the grounds, except the walk you had from the railway station?”

“No.”

“You didn’t walk around the house to admire the﻿—er﻿—architecture?”

“No, I did not. I was only in the house a few minutes, and then I drove away.”

===Chinese===

“除了从火车站走过来那一程，您可曾进宅园里散过步？”

“没有。”

“没绕着宅子兜上一圈，观赏观赏那——呃——建筑式样？”

“没有，没兜过。我在宅子里统共只待了几分钟，随后就坐车走了。”

===Original===

Mr. Parr raised his eyes to the ceiling.

“Would it be asking you too much,” he demanded apologetically, “if I requested you to show me the patent shoes you wore that day?”

“Certainly,” said Marl, rising with alacrity.

===Chinese===

帕尔先生抬眼望着天花板。

“是不是强您所难了，”他带着歉意问道，“劳您把那天穿的漆皮鞋拿出来给我瞧瞧？”

“当然可以。”马尔说着，欣然起身。

===Original===

He was out of the room a few minutes, and came back with a pair of long pointed patent boots.

The detective took them in his hand and looked earnestly at the sole.

“Yes,” he said. “Of course, these are not the boots you were wearing, because﻿—” he rubbed the soles gently with his hand, “there is dust on them, and the ground has been wet for the last week.”

===Chinese===

他出屋去了几分钟，回来时捧着一双长尖头的漆皮靴。

侦探把靴子接在手里，一本正经地细看靴底。

“唔，”他说，“当然，这不是您那天穿的靴子，因为——”他用手轻轻擦了擦靴底，“这上头是尘土，而这一礼拜来，地面一直是湿的。”

===Original===

Marl’s heart nearly stopped beating.

“Those are the boots I wore,” he said defiantly. “What you call ‘dust’ is really dried mud.”

Parr looked at his dusty fingers and shook his head.

“I think there must be some mistake, Mr. Marl,” he said gently. “This is chalk dust.” He put the boots down and rose. “However, it isn’t very important,” he said. He stood so long, looking down at the carpet, that Mr. Marl, in spite of his fear, became impatient.

===Chinese===

马尔的心脏险些停了跳。

“那就是我穿的靴子。”他梗着脖子顶了一句，“你管它叫‘尘土’的那玩意儿，其实是干掉的泥。”

帕尔瞧了瞧自己沾灰的手指，摇了摇头。

“我看这里头多半有点误会，马尔先生。”他温和地说，“这是白垩粉。”他把靴子放下，站起身来。“不过，这事也无关紧要。”他说。他站在那儿，低头望着地毯，望了那么久，马尔先生尽管心怀恐惧，也终于等得不耐烦了。

===Original===

“Is there anything more I can do for you, officer?” he asked.

“Yes,” said Parr. “I want you to give me the name and address of your tailor. Perhaps you would write it down for me.”

“My tailor?” Mr. Marl glared at the visitor. “What the dickens do you want of my tailor?” And then, with a laugh, “Well, you are a curious man, inspector; but I’ll do it with pleasure.”

===Chinese===

“还有什么要我效劳的吗，警官？”他问。

“有，”帕尔说，“我想请您把您裁缝的姓名和地址给我。劳驾写下来吧。”

“我的裁缝？”马尔先生瞪着来客，“你到底要我的裁缝做什么？”跟着又笑了一声：“好吧，你这人可真古怪，探长；不过这忙我乐意帮。”

===Original===

He went to his secretaire, pulled out a sheet of paper, wrote down a name and address and, blotting it, handed it to the detective.

“Thank you, sir.”

Parr did not even look at the address, but put the paper into his pocket.

===Chinese===

他走到写字台前，抽出一张纸，写下一个姓名和地址，用吸墨纸压了压，递给侦探。

“多谢您，先生。”

帕尔对那地址连看也没看一眼，把纸片塞进了口袋。

===Original===

“I’m sorry to bother you, but you will realise that everybody who was present at the house within twenty-four hours of Mr. Beardmore’s death must necessarily be interrogated. The Crimson Circle﻿—”

“The Crimson Circle!” gasped Mr. Marl, and the detective looked at him straightly.

“Didn’t you know that the Crimson Circle were responsible for this murder?”

===Chinese===

“抱歉叨扰您。不过您也想得到，比德莫尔先生死后二十四小时之内到过那宅子的每一个人，都免不了要受一番盘问。红圈——”

“红圈！”马尔先生倒抽一口冷气，侦探目光笔直地盯住了他。

“您不知道这桩凶案是红圈干的？”

===Original===

To do him justice, Mr. Felix Marl knew nothing of the kind. He had seen a brief report that James Beardmore had been found shot but the association of the murder with the Crimson Circle had not been disclosed except by the *Monitor*, a newspaper which Mr. Marl never read.

He dropped into a chair, quaking.

“The Crimson Circle,” he muttered. “Good God﻿—I never thought﻿—” he checked himself.

“What didn’t you think?” asked Parr gently.

“The Crimson Circle,” murmured the big man again. “I thought it was just a﻿—” he did not complete his sentence.

===Chinese===

平心而论，菲利克斯·马尔先生对此当真一无所知。他见过一则简讯，说詹姆斯·比德莫尔被人发现中弹身亡；但凶案同红圈的干系，除了《晨哨报》以外尚无一家报纸披露——那份报纸，马尔先生是从来不读的。

他一屁股跌进椅子里，瑟瑟发抖。

“红圈，”他喃喃道，“老天爷——我从来没想过——”他生生把话煞住了。

“没想过什么？”帕尔轻声问。

“红圈。”大个子又低声念了一遍。“我只当它不过是个——”这句他没有说完。

===Original===

For an hour after the detective’s departure Felix Marl sat huddled up in his chair, his head in his hands.

The Crimson Circle!

It was the first time he had ever been brought into even the remotest touch with that blackmailing organisation, and now its obtrusion upon the order of his thoughts was so violent that it disturbed every theory he had formed.

“I don’t like it,” he muttered as he got up painfully and turned on the light in the darkened room. “I think this is where I get away.”

He spent the evening examining his bankbook, and the examination was very comforting. He could squeeze out a little more, he thought, and then﻿—

===Chinese===

侦探告辞之后，菲利克斯·马尔蜷缩在椅子里，双手抱着头，足足坐了一个钟头。

红圈！

他生平头一遭，哪怕是隔着十万八千里，同这个敲诈组织沾上了边；如今它这样蛮横地闯进他思绪的秩序里，把他原先打好的全部算盘都搅了个七零八落。

“这局面我不喜欢。”他吃力地站起身，在黑下来的房间里拧亮电灯，一面咕哝，“我看，是我抽身的时候了。”

这一晚他都花在核对银行存折上，查完的结果颇能宽心。还可以再挤出一点儿来，他寻思，到那时——

## XIII / 十三

===Original===
Mr. Marl Squeezes a Little More

===Chinese===
马尔先生再榨一把

===Original===
Another agent of the Crimson Circle found her lines cast in pleasant places. She had been accepted by Mr. Brabazon without question, and evidently the man in the car possessed extraordinary influences.

What was even more extraordinary was that day followed day without a word from her mysterious employer. She had expected that he would almost immediately avail himself of her services, but she had been at Brabazon’s (late Seller’s) Bank nearly a month before she received any communication. It came one morning. She found the letter on her desk, addressed in bold pen-print.

There was no sign of the Circle on the letter, which began without preamble:

===Chinese===
红圈安插的又一名密使，这一回落得了个称心的去处。她未受半点盘问，便被布拉巴宗先生接纳——显而易见，汽车里那位先生的神通非同小可。

更蹊跷的是，日子一天天过去，那位神秘的雇主竟音信全无。她原以为他差不多立刻就会用得上她，可她进了布拉巴宗银行——也就是从前的塞勒银行（Seller’s Bank）——将近一个月，才头一回收到任何音讯。信是一天早晨来的。她发现信就搁在自己桌上，信封上的地址以粗重的钢笔印刷体写就。

信上不见红圈的任何标记，开篇便直截了当：

===Original===
Make the acquaintance of Marl. Discover why he has a hold over Brabazon. Send me the figures of his account and notify me immediately his account is closed. Notify me also if Parr and Derrick Yale come to the bank. Wire Johnson, 23, Mildred Street, City.

She carried out her instructions faithfully, though it was not for a few days that she had an opportunity of seeing Mr. Marl.

Only once did Derrick Yale come into the bank. She had seen him before, when he was a guest of the Beardmores, and even if she had not, she would have recognised him from the portrait of the famous detective which had appeared in the newspapers.

===Chinese===
结识马尔。查明他凭什么拿捏布拉巴宗。把他账户的数目报给我；他一销户，立即通知我。帕尔与德里克·耶尔若到银行来，也须禀报。电告约翰逊（Johnson），伦敦城米尔德丽德街（Mildred Street）23 号。

她忠实地执行了这些指令，只是过了好几天，才得到见到马尔先生的机会。

德里克·耶尔只到银行里来过一回。她从前见过他——那会儿他是比德莫尔家的客人；就算没见过，单凭报上登过的那位名探的肖像，她也认得出他来。

===Original===
What his business was she did not learn, but, looking out of the corner of her eye from the little office she occupied alone, by virtue of her position as Brabazon’s private secretary, she saw him talking with one of the tellers at the counter, and duly notified the Crimson Circle.

Inspector Parr, however, did not come, nor did she see Jack Beardmore. She did not want to think too much of Jack. He was not a pleasant subject.

===Chinese===
他来办的是什么事，她无从得知；但她身为布拉巴宗的私人秘书，独自占着一间小办公室，从眼角里觑见他在柜台前同一个出纳员说话，事后便如期通报了红圈。

然而帕尔探长始终没有露面，杰克·比德莫尔她也无缘见到。她不愿对杰克多费心思。他可不是个叫人愉快的话题。

---

===Original===
In moments of perturbation John Brabazon, the austere and stately president of Seller’s Bank, had a characteristic little trick. His white hands would stray to the hair, curly and thick at the back of his head. One curl he would twist about his forefinger for a moment, and then he would slowly bring the tips of his fingers across his bald dome until they rested on his forehead. In such moments, with his head bowed and his fingers resting on his brow, he had the appearance of being engaged in prayer.

The gentleman who sat with him in his neat office had no characteristics at all. He was a big man, who breathed noisily, and he was puffy with lazy, indulgent living, but he did not fidget and his hands were folded over his large waistcoat.

===Chinese===
塞勒银行那位冷峻威严的行长约翰·布拉巴宗，每逢心烦意乱，总要摆弄一个招牌式的小动作：他那双白皙的手会不知不觉挪向后脑的头发——那里依旧生得又浓又卷。他会把一绺卷发往食指上绕上片刻，再让指尖缓缓掠过光秃的头顶，直到停在额头上。每逢这种时刻，他垂着头，指头抵着眉心，那模样活像正在祷告。

陪他坐在那间整洁办公室里的这位绅士，却毫无特色可言。他是个大块头，呼吸粗重，慵懒而放纵的生活把他养得浑身虚胖；不过他并不躁动，只把双手交叠着搭在那件宽大的马甲上。

===Original===
“My dear Marl,” the banker’s voice was soft and almost caressing, “you try my patience at times. I will say nothing about the strain you put upon my resources.”

The big man chuckled.

“I give you security, Brab﻿—excellent security, old man. You can’t deny that!”

Mr. Brabazon’s white fingers played a tune on the edge of his desk.

“You bring me impossible schemes, and hitherto I have been foolish enough to finance them,” he said. “There must come an end to such folly. You have no need for help. Your balance at this bank alone is nearly a hundred thousand.”

===Chinese===
“亲爱的马尔，”银行家的嗓音轻柔，近乎温存，“你有时候真会考验我的耐性。你把我的资金耗损成了什么样，我就一字不提了。”

大块头呵呵直笑。

“我给你的是抵押担保，布拉布——顶呱呱的担保，老伙计。这你可否认不了！”

布拉巴宗先生白皙的手指在桌沿上敲敲点点，弹出一支曲子的节拍。

“你拿来的净是些行不通的方案，而迄今为止，我一直糊涂得出钱替你张罗，”他说，“这样的荒唐事总得有个了结。你根本用不着人接济。单是你存在本行的存款，就将近十万镑。”

===Original===
Marl looked round at the door and bent forward.

“I’ll tell you a story,” he mumbled, “a story about a penniless young clerk that married the widow of Seller, of Seller’s Bank. She was old enough to be his mother, and died suddenly﻿—in Switzerland. She fell over a precipice. Don’t I know it? Wasn’t I takin’ photographs of the bee-utiful mountain scenery? Did I ever show you the picture of that accident, Brab? You are in it! Yes, you’re in it, though you told the examining magistrate you were miles and miles away!”

Mr. Brabazon’s eyes were on the desk. Not a muscle of his face moved.

===Chinese===
马尔回头望了望门口，随即俯身凑近。

“我给你讲个故事吧，”他咕哝道，“讲一个身无分文的年轻小职员，娶了塞勒银行那位塞勒的遗孀。那寡妇的年纪给他当娘都有富余，后来死得突然——死在瑞士。她是坠崖死的。这我能不知道么？当时我不正在拍那‘美——啊——丽’的山间风景么？出事那天的照片，我给你瞧过没有，布拉布？相片里可有你在内！不错，你就在里头——尽管你跟预审法官说，自己远在几十英里之外！”

布拉巴宗先生的眼睛望着桌面。他脸上没有一块肌肉动弹。

===Original===
“Besides,” said Mr. Marl in a more normal tone, “you can afford it. You’re making another matrimonial alliance﻿—that’s the expression, ain’t it?”

The banker raised his eyes and frowned at his visitor.

“What do you mean?” he demanded.

Mr. Marl was evidently amused. He slapped his knee and choked with laughter.

“What about the person you met in Steyne Square the other night﻿—the one in the closed motorcar, eh? Don’t deny it! I saw you! A nice little car, it was.”

===Chinese===
“再说了，”马尔先生换上较为正常的口气说道，“你出得起这笔钱。你这不是又要缔结一桩‘婚姻同盟’了么——文雅点儿的说法，对吧？”

银行家抬起眼睛，冲来访者皱起眉头。

“你这是什么意思？”他质问道。

马尔先生显然乐不可支。他一巴掌拍在膝盖上，笑得直呛。

“前几天夜里你在斯泰恩广场见的那位又怎么说——坐在那辆封闭小汽车里的，嗯？别想抵赖！我亲眼看见你了！好一辆漂亮的小车。”

===Original===
Now, for the first time, Brabazon displayed signs of emotion. His face was grey and drawn and his eyes seemed to have receded further into their sockets.

“I will arrange your loan,” he said.

Mr. Marl’s expression of satisfaction was interrupted by a knock at the door. At Brabazon’s “Come in,” the door opened to admit one whose appearance put all other matters out of the visitor’s head.

The girl brought a paper which she placed before her employer﻿—evidently a pencilled telephone message.

===Chinese===
直到此刻，布拉巴宗才头一回露出激动的迹象。他的脸灰败而紧绷，一双眼睛仿佛更深地陷进了眼窝。

“你的贷款，我会安排。”他说。

马尔先生那副志得意满的神情，被一阵叩门声打断了。布拉巴宗应了声“进来”，门开了，走进来一个人——来者的模样，登时把客人心里其余的念头一扫而空。

姑娘拿着一张纸进来，放到雇主面前——显然是用铅笔记下的一通电话留言。

===Original===
“White﻿—gold﻿—red,” Mr. Marl’s senses registered the impression he received. White, creamy white and delicate skin, red as poppies the scarlet lips, yellow as ripe corn the hair. He saw her in profile, was revolted a little at the firmness of her chin﻿—Mr. Marl liked women who were yielding and soft and malleable in his hands﻿—but the beauty of mouth and nose and brow﻿—they made him blink.

He breathed a little more quickly, a little more loudly, and when she had gone after a colloquy, in a low tone, he sighed.

“What a queen!” he said. “I’ve seen her somewhere before. What is her name?”

“Drummond﻿—Thalia Drummond,” said Mr. Brabazon, eyeing the gross man coldly.

===Chinese===
“白——金——红。”马尔先生的感官把得到的印象逐一登记在册。白色，是乳白细腻的肌肤；红色，是罂粟般猩红的双唇；黄色，是熟麦般灿黄的一头头发。他看到的是她的侧脸，那下巴的坚定劲儿叫他微微生厌——马尔先生喜欢的是温顺、绵软、任凭他揉搓的女人——可那嘴唇、鼻子和眉额的秀美，却叫他直眨眼睛。

他的呼吸急促了些，也粗重了些；她和雇主低声交谈几句之后便退了出去，他这才叹了口气。

“好一位女王！”他说，“我从前在哪儿见过她。她叫什么名字？”

“德拉蒙德——塔莉娅·德拉蒙德。”布拉巴宗先生说，冷冷地打量着这个臃肿粗俗的家伙。

===Original===
“Thalia Drummond!” repeated Felix slowly. “Isn’t she the girl who used to be with Froyant? Bit sweet on her yourself, eh, Brabazon?”

The man at the writing-table looked at the other steadily.

“I do not make it a practice to be ‘sweet on’ my employees, Mr. Marl,” he said. “Miss Drummond is a very efficient worker. That is all that I require of my staff.”

===Chinese===
“塔莉娅·德拉蒙德！”菲利克斯慢条斯理地重复了一遍，“她不就是从前跟着弗鲁瓦扬做事的那个姑娘么？你自己有点看上她了吧，嗯，布拉巴宗？”

坐在写字台前的人目不转睛地瞧着对方。

“我向来没有‘看上’自家雇员的习惯，马尔先生，”他说，“德拉蒙德小姐做事极为干练。我对属下的全部要求，仅此而已。”

===Original===
Marl rose heavily, chuckling.

“I’ll see you tomorrow morning about that other business,” he said.

He laughed wheezily, but Mr. Brabazon did not smile.

“At half-past ten tomorrow,” he said, going to the door with the visitor. “Or can you make it eleven?”

===Chinese===
马尔拖着沉重的身子站起身来，嘴里还嘿嘿地笑。

“另一桩事，我明天上午来找你谈。”他说。

他呼哧带喘地笑着，布拉巴宗先生却没有一丝笑意。

“明天十点半，”他说着，送客人到门口，“要不改成十一点，你看行么？”

===Original===
“Eleven,” agreed the man.

“Good morning,” said the banker, but did not offer his hand.

Hardly had the door closed on the visitor before Mr. Brabazon locked it and returned to his desk. He took from his pocketbook a plain white card, and dipping his pen in the red ink, drew a small circle. Beneath he wrote the words:

Felix Marl saw our interview in Steyne Square. He lives at 79, Marisburg Place.

He put the card into an envelope and addressed it:

Mr. Johnson, 23, Mildred Street, City.

===Chinese===
“十一点。”那人应承道。

“早安。”银行家说，却没有伸手。

客人刚出门，门一合上，布拉巴宗先生便锁了门，回到写字台前。他从记事夹里取出一张素白卡片，笔尖蘸了红墨水，画下一个小圆圈，然后在下面写道：

菲利克斯·马尔目睹了我们在斯泰恩广场的会面。他住在马里斯堡广场（Marisburg Place）79 号。

他把卡片装进一只信封，在外面写上收件人：

约翰逊先生，伦敦城米尔德丽德街 23 号。

## XIV / 十四

===Original===

Thalia Is Asked Out

===Chinese===

有人约塔莉娅

===Original===

Mr. Marl had to pass through the bank premises, and he glanced along the two rows of desks without, however, catching a glimpse of the girl whose face he sought. Near the end of the counter was a small compartment, the occupant of which was shielded from observation by opaque glass windows. The door was ajar, and he caught just a flash of the figure and walked toward the door. A girl at a typewriter watched him curiously.

Thalia Drummond looked up from her desk to see the big smiling face of a man looking down at her.

“Busy, Miss Drummond?”

“Very,” she replied, but did not seem to resent his intrusion.

“Don’t get much fun here, do you?” he asked.

===Chinese===

马尔先生不得不从银行的营业场地里穿过，他顺着两排办公桌扫视过去，却没能瞥见他寻觅的那张面孔。柜台近尽头处有一间小隔间，里面的人叫不透明的玻璃窗遮着，外面瞧不见。隔间的门虚掩着，他恰好闪见一道身影，便朝那扇门走去。一个坐在打字机跟前的姑娘好奇地打量着他。

塔莉娅·德拉蒙德从案头上抬起眼来，只见一张堆着笑的男人大脸正低头打量自己。

“忙吗，德拉蒙德小姐？”

“忙得很。”她答道，对他的唐突闯入却似乎并不见怪。

“在这地方捞不着多少乐子吧？”他问。

===Original===

“Not a lot.” Her dark eyes were surveying him appraisingly.

“What about a bit of dinner one of these nights and a show to follow?” he asked.

Her eyes took him in from his dyed hair to his painfully varnished boots.

“You’re a wicked old man,” she said calmly, “but dinner is my favourite meal.”

His grin broadened and the fires of conquest flickered in his faded eyes.

===Chinese===

“捞不着多少。”她那双黑眼睛正上上下下地打量他，暗自称量。

“这几晚挑一晚，一块儿吃顿饭，完了再看场戏，怎么样？”他问。

她的目光把他从头到脚收了一遍——从那头染过的头发，到那双擦得扎眼的漆皮靴子。

“你这老不正经的。”她平平静静地说，“不过晚饭嘛，倒是我顶爱的一顿。”

他嘴上的笑容咧得更大了，那双黯淡的眼睛里，征服的欲火忽闪着蹿了起来。

===Original===

“What about ‘The Moulin Gris’?” He suggested the restaurant, without doubting her acceptance, but her lips curled scornfully.

“Why not at Hooligans Fish Parlour?” she asked. “No, it’s the Ritz-Carlton or nothing for me.”

Mr. Marl was staggered, but pleased.

“You’re a princess,” he beamed, “and you shall have a royal feed! What about tonight?”

She nodded.

===Chinese===

“‘灰磨坊’（Moulin Gris）怎么样？”他提议了那家馆子，对她准会应承毫不存疑，谁知她的嘴角轻蔑地撇了下去。

“干吗不去‘流氓鱼馆’（Hooligans Fish Parlour）？”她问，“不行，要请我，就得是丽思卡尔顿（Ritz-Carlton），否则免谈。”

马尔先生被唬了一跳，心里却是欢喜的。

“您真是位公主，”他喜笑颜开，“保准请您吃一顿御膳！今儿晚上怎么样？”

她点了点头。

===Original===

“Meet me at my house in Marisburg Place, Bayswater Road. 7:30. You’ll find my name on the door.”

He paused, expecting her to demur, but to his surprise, she nodded again.

“Goodbye, darling,” said the bold Mr. Marl and kissed the tips of his fat fingers.

“Shut the door,” said the girl and went on with her work.

===Chinese===

“到我家来碰头——贝斯沃特路（Bayswater Road）的马里斯堡广场。七点半。门上有我的名字，一找就着。”

他顿住话头，只当她要推辞，不料她竟又点了点头，倒叫他吃了一惊。

“再会，宝贝儿。”这位胆大包天的马尔先生说着，吻了吻自己胖手指的指尖。

“把门带上。”姑娘说罢，接着干她的活。

===Original===

She was destined again to be interrupted. This time the visitor was a good-looking girl, whose forearms were gauntletted in shiny leather. It was the typist who had followed Mr. Marl’s movements with such curiosity.

Thalia leant back in her chair as the newcomer carefully closed the door behind her and sat down.

“Well, Macroy, what’s biting you?” she asked inelegantly.

The words did not seem to harmonise with the delicate refinement of face, and not for the first time did Milly Macroy look at the girl wonderingly.

===Chinese===

她注定还要再被打断一回。这回进来的访客是个模样俊俏的姑娘，两条小臂套在亮闪闪的皮长手套里。正是方才那么好奇地盯着马尔先生一举一动的那个打字员。

新来的姑娘小心翼翼地带上身后那扇门，坐了下来；塔莉娅往椅背上一靠。

“喂，麦克罗伊，什么事咬着你了？”她问得毫不斯文。

这话同那张脸上精致的秀气全不相称；米莉·麦克罗伊又纳罕地打量起这位姑娘——这也不是头一回了。

===Original===

“Who’s the old nut?” she asked.

“An admirer,” replied Thalia calmly.

“You do attract ’em, kid,” commented Milly Macroy, with some envy, and there was a little pause.

“Well?” asked Thalia. “You haven’t come here to discuss my amours, have you?”

Milly smiled furtively.

“If amours is French for boys, I haven’t,” she said. “I’ve come to have a straight talk with you, Drummond.”

===Chinese===

“那老怪物是谁？”她问。

“一个崇拜者。”塔莉娅若无其事地答道。

“你可真能招惹这些人，丫头。”米莉·麦克罗伊带着几分醋意评了一句，随后静了片刻。

“怎么？”塔莉娅问，“你总不至于跑到这儿来，是要谈论我的风流韵事吧？”

米莉诡秘地一笑。

“要是 amours 这个法国字就是‘男人’的意思，那我不是。”她说，“我是来找你打开天窗说亮话的，德拉蒙德。”

===Original===

“Straight talks are meat and drink to me,” said Thalia Drummond.

“Do you remember the money that went out by registered post last Friday to the Sellinger Corporation?”

Thalia nodded.

“Well, I suppose you know that they claim that when the package arrived it contained nothing but paper?”

===Chinese===

“直来直去的谈话，于我就是茶饭家常。”塔莉娅·德拉蒙德说。

“上星期五经挂号邮寄给塞林格公司（Sellinger Corporation）的那笔款子，你还记得吧？”

塔莉娅点了点头。

“唔，想必你知道了：他们咬定包裹寄到的时候，里头装的东西除了纸什么也没有？”

===Original===

“Is that so?” asked Thalia. “Mr. Brabazon has said nothing to me about it,” and she returned the other’s scrutinising glance without faltering.

“I packed that money in the envelope,” said Milly Macroy slowly, “and you had it to check. There’s only you and me in this business, Miss Drummond, and one of us pinched the money, and I’ll swear it wasn’t me.”

“Then it must be me,” said Thalia with an innocent smile. “Really, Macroy, that’s a fairly serious accusation to make against an innocent female.”

The admiration in Milly’s eyes increased.

“You’re a Thorough-Bad, if ever there was one!” she said. “Now, look here, kid, let’s put all our cards on the table. A month ago, soon after you came to the bank, there was a hundred note missing from the Foreign Exchange desk.”

===Chinese===

“是吗？”塔莉娅问，“这件事布拉巴宗先生对我只字未提。”说罢她迎住对方审视的目光，眼皮都不曾颤一颤。

“那笔钱是我亲手装进信封的，”米莉·麦克罗伊慢吞吞地说，“复核的差事在你手里。这桩事里头只有你我两个人，德拉蒙德小姐，咱们俩总有一个把款子昧下了——我敢起誓，那个人不是我。”

“那就必定是我咯。”塔莉娅说着，挂起一脸无辜的微笑，“说真的，麦克罗伊，往一个清白女人身上安这样一条罪名，分量可不轻。”

米莉眼里的钦佩又添了几分。

“你可真是个纯种的坏种，天底下头一份！”她说，“喂，你听好，丫头，咱们把牌全摊到桌面上来。一个月以前，你刚进银行那阵子，外汇柜台上短了一张一百镑的票子。”

===Original===

“Well?” asked Thalia when she paused.

“Well, I happen to know that you had it and that it was changed by you at Bilbury’s in the Strand. I can tell you the number if you want to know.”

Thalia swung round and looked at the other under lowered brows.

“What have we here?” she asked in mock consternation. “A female sleuth! Heavens, I am indeed undone!”

The extravagant mockery of it all took Milly aback.

“You’ve got ice in your brain!” she said. She leant forward and laid her hand on the girl’s arm. “There may be trouble over this Sellinger business, and you will want all the friends you can get.”

===Chinese===

“后来呢？”见她住了口，塔莉娅问。

“后来，我偏偏知道那张票子进了你的腰包，还是你拿到斯特兰德街（the Strand）的比尔伯里（Bilbury’s）兑开的。你要想知道票号，我背给你听。”

塔莉娅霍地转过身，压着眉梢打量对方。

“这是什么阵仗？”她装出一副大惊小怪的腔调问道，“一位女侦探！天哪，我这下可真完了！”

这一番夸张透顶的挖苦，反倒把米莉怔住了。

“你的脑浆是冰做的！”她说着，俯身向前，一只手搭上姑娘的胳膊，“这桩塞林格的事说不定要出乱子，到了那一步，你能拉拢多少朋友，就得拉拢多少。”

===Original===

“So will you, for the matter of that,” said Thalia coolly. “You handled the money.”

“And you took it,” said the other, in a matter-of-fact tone. “Don’t let us have any argument about it, Drummond. If we stick together there’ll be no trouble at all﻿—I can swear that the envelope was sealed in my presence and that the money was there.”

There was a dancing light of amusement in Thalia Drummond’s eyes and she laughed silently.

“All right,” she said, with a little shrug of her shoulders. “Let it go at that. Now, I suppose, having saved me from ruin, you’re going to ask me a favour? I’ll set your mind at rest about the money. I took it because I had a good home for it. I need money frequently and anyway there have been lots of postal robberies lately. There was a long article in the paper about it the other day. Now go ahead.”

Milly Macroy, who had not a slight acquaintance with the criminal classes, stared at the girl in amazement.

“You’re ice all right,” she nodded, “but you’ve got to cut out this cheap pilfering, otherwise you’re liable to spoil a real big thing and I can’t afford to see it spoilt. If you want a share of big money you’ve got to come in with people who are working big﻿—do you get that?”

===Chinese===

“论起这一节，你也一样。”塔莉娅冷冷地说，“经手那笔钱的是你。”

“下手拿走的是你。”对方一副就事论事的腔调，“这上头咱们不必多掰扯，德拉蒙德。只要咱们俩抱成团，就什么事也没有——我可以发誓，信封是当着我的面封上的，钱就在里头。”

塔莉娅·德拉蒙德眼里跳动着一点亮晶晶的笑意，无声地笑了。

“好吧，”她说，微微耸了耸肩，“就照这话收场。这么说，你既然救我免于倾家荡产，接下来就要请我帮个忙了吧？关于那笔钱，我先给你吃颗定心丸：我拿它，是因为我给它寻着了个好下家。我时常要钱使，再说近来邮政劫案多得很，前几天报上还登了长长一篇呢。现在，说你的吧。”

米莉·麦克罗伊对犯罪这路人绝非略知一二，此刻却直瞪着这姑娘，惊得说不出话。

“你这人真是块冰，没跑了。”她点点头，“可这种上不得台面的小偷小摸，你必须罢手，不然保不齐要毁掉一桩真正的大买卖，那样的损失我可担待不起。你想分大钱，就得同干大票的人搭伙——这话你明白？”

===Original===

“I get it,” said Thalia, “and who are your collaborators?”

Miss Macroy did not recognise the term but answered discreetly:

“There’s a gentleman I know﻿—”

“Say ‘man,’ ” said Thalia. “Gentleman always reminds me of a tailor’s ad.”

“Well, a man if you like,” said the patient Miss Macroy. “He’s a friend of mine and he’s been watching you for a week or two, and he thinks you’re the kind of clever girl who might make a lot of money without trouble. I told him about the other affair and he wants to see you.”

===Chinese===

“明白，”塔莉娅说，“那么你的同伙都是哪些人？”

麦克罗伊小姐没吃透这个字眼，回答得倒还谨慎：

“我认识一位先生——”

“说‘男人’。”塔莉娅说，“‘先生’这个词儿总叫我想起裁缝铺的广告。”

“好吧，依你说，一个男人。”耐着性子的麦克罗伊小姐说，“他是我一个朋友，把你的动静盯了一两个礼拜啦。他掂量着，你正是那种不用惹麻烦也能挣大钱的伶俐姑娘。先前那桩事我告诉他了，他要见见你。”

===Original===

“Another admirer?” asked Thalia Drummond with a lift of her perfect eyebrows, and Macroy’s face darkened.

“There’ll be none of that, you understand, Drummond,” she said decisively. “This fellow and I are sort of﻿—engaged.”

“Heaven forbid,” said Thalia Drummond piously, “that I should come between two loving hearts.”

“And you needn’t be sarcastic either,” said Macroy, redder still. “I tell you that there’s to be no lovey-dovey stuff in this. It’s real business, you understand?”

Thalia played with her paper-knife. Presently she asked:

===Chinese===

“又一位崇拜者？”塔莉娅·德拉蒙德问着，两道完美无瑕的秀眉往上一挑。麦克罗伊的脸沉了下来。

“这种事一概不许有，你听明白，德拉蒙德。”她斩钉截铁地说，“这个家伙同我算是——订了婚的。”

“皇天在上，”塔莉娅·德拉蒙德一脸虔敬地说，“断断不容我插到两颗恩爱的心中间去。”

“你也犯不着阴阳怪气。”麦克罗伊说，脸涨得更红了，“我把话撂明：这里头不许有卿卿我我那一套。这是正经过的买卖，你懂么？”

塔莉娅摆弄着裁纸刀。过了片刻，她问：

===Original===

“Suppose I don’t want to come into your combination?”

Milly Macroy looked suspiciously at the girl.

“Come and have a bit of dinner after the bank closes,” she said.

“Nothing but invitations to dinner,” murmured Thalia and the nimble-witted Milly Macroy jumped at the truth.

“The old boy asked you to dinner, did he?” she demanded. “Well, ain’t that luck!” She whistled and her eyes brightened. She was about to offer a confidence, but changed her mind. “He’s got loads of money out of money-lending. My dear, I can see you with a diamond necklace in a week or two!”

===Chinese===

“要是我偏不愿入你们的伙呢？”

米莉·麦克罗伊狐疑地瞧着这姑娘。

“等银行关门，来一块儿吃顿便饭吧。”她说。

“净是请吃饭的约。”塔莉娅低声咕哝。机灵透顶的米莉·麦克罗伊一下子就扑到了真相上。

“那老小子开口请你吃饭了，对不对？”她追问，“嘿，这运气可不就来了！”她打了个呼哨，眼睛也亮了。她正要掏一句心腹话，又转了念头。“他放债放出了大把大把的钱。亲爱的，我都瞧得见，再过一两个礼拜，你脖子上就挂起钻石项链啦！”

===Original===

Thalia straightened herself and took up her pen.

“Pearls are my weakness,” she said. “All right, Macroy, I’ll see you tonight,” and she went on working.

Milly Macroy lingered.

“Look here, you’re not going to tell this gentleman what I said about my being engaged to him, are you?”

“There’s Brab’s bell,” said Thalia, rising and taking up her notebook as a buzzer sounded. “No, I’m not going to discuss anything of the kind﻿—I hate fairy stories anyway.”

===Chinese===

塔莉娅坐直身子，拿起了笔。

“珍珠才是我的软肋。”她说，“行，麦克罗伊，今晚见。”说罢接着办公。

米莉·麦克罗伊还磨蹭着不走。

“喂，我说跟他订婚的那句话，你不会去学给那位先生听吧？”

“布拉巴宗打铃了。”蜂音器一响，塔莉娅便起身拿起记事本，“不会的，这类事我一概不谈——反正我顶腻烦童话故事。”

===Original===

Miss Macroy looked after the retreating figure of the girl with an expression which was not friendly.

Mr. Brabazon was sitting at his desk when the girl came in, and handed her a sealed envelope.

“Send this by hand,” he said.

Thalia looked at the address and nodded, and then looked at Mr. Brabazon with a new interest. Truly the Crimson Circle was recruited from many and various classes.

===Chinese===

麦克罗伊小姐望着那姑娘走出去的背影，脸上那副神情可算不上友善。

姑娘进去时，布拉巴宗先生正坐在书桌前；他递给她一封封好的信。

“这封派专人送去。”他说。

塔莉娅看了看地址，点头应下，随即又带着一分新的兴味，端详起布拉巴宗先生来。红圈招募的班底，果然取自三教九流，各色人等无所不包。

## XV / 十五

===Original===
Thalia Joins the Gang

===Chinese===

塔莉娅入伙

===Original===
Thalia Drummond was almost the last of the staff to leave the bank that night, and she stood on the steps looking idly from left to right as she pulled on her gloves. If she saw the man who was watching her from the opposite side of the road she did not reveal the fact by so much as a glance. Presently her eyes lighted upon Milly waiting a few yards up the street, and she walked toward her.

“You’ve been a long time, Drummond,” grumbled Miss Macroy. “You mustn’t keep my friend waiting, you know. He doesn’t like it.”

“He’ll get over that,” said Thalia. “I do not run to timetable where men are concerned.”

She fell in by Milly’s side and they walked a hundred yards along the busy thoroughfare before they turned into Reeder Street.

===Chinese===

那天夜里，塔莉娅·德拉蒙德差不多是最后一个离开银行的职员。她站在台阶上，一面往手上套手套，一面漫不经意地左右张望。马路对面有个男人正盯着她，她纵然看见了，也连一瞥都不曾流露。不一会儿，她望见米莉在街那头几步开外候着，便朝她走去。

“你可真磨蹭，德拉蒙德。”麦克罗伊小姐嘟囔道，“要知道，不该让我那位朋友干等。他不喜欢等人。”

“他会习惯的。”塔莉娅说，“在男人身上，我从不按时刻表行事。”

她跟上米莉，并肩沿热闹的大街走了一百来码，才拐进里德街（Reeder Street）。

===Original===
The restaurants in Reeder Street have taken to themselves names which are designed to suggest the gaiety and epicurean wonders of Paris. The “Moulin Gris” was a small, deep shop which, with the aid of numerous mirrors and the application of gold leaf, had managed to create an atmosphere of cramped splendour.

The tables were set for dinner and empty, for it was two hours before the meal, and to the proprietors of the “Moulin Gris” such a function as afternoon tea was unknown. They went up a narrow stairway to another dining-room on the first floor, and a man who was seated at one of the tables rose briskly to meet them. He was a sleek, dark, young man, his beautifully brilliantined hair was brushed back from his forehead, and he was dressed, if not in the height of fashion, at least in the height of the fashion which he favoured.

A faint odour of *l’origan*, a soft large hand, a pair of bright unwinking eyes, were the first impressions which Thalia received.

“Sit down, sit down, Miss Drummond,” he said brightly. “Waiter, bring that tea.”

===Chinese===

里德街上的餐馆纷纷给自己起了些名号，专意教人联想到巴黎的旖旎风流与口腹盛境。“灰磨坊”便是其中一间窄而深的小铺面，凭着一面又一面镜子和一层又一层金箔，居然造出了一派局促的富丽气象。

餐桌上摆好了晚餐餐具，却空无一人——离开饭还有两个钟头，而在“灰磨坊”的东家这里，下午茶这等名目是闻所未闻的。两人顺一道窄楼梯上了二楼的另一间餐室，一个坐在桌旁的男人利落起身相迎。他是个油头粉面的黑发青年，抹得油光水滑的头发从额头向后梳去；他的穿戴即便算不得天下最时髦，至少也是他所中意的那一路时髦里的登峰造极。

一缕牛至香水（l’origan）的淡香，一只绵软的大手，一双亮而不眨的眼睛——这便是塔莉娅得到的第一印象。

“坐，坐，德拉蒙德小姐。”他满面生辉地说，“侍者，上茶。”

===Original===
“This is Thalia Drummond,” said Miss Macroy, unnecessarily it seemed.

“We needn’t be introduced,” laughed the young man. “I’ve heard a lot about you, Miss Drummond. My name’s Barnet.”

“ ‘Flush’ Barnet,” said Thalia, and he seemed surprised and not ill-pleased.

“You’ve heard of me, have you?”

“She’s heard of everything,” said Miss Macroy in resignation, “and what’s more,” she added significantly, “she knows Marl, and is dining with him tonight.”

===Chinese===

“这位是塔莉娅·德拉蒙德。”麦克罗伊小姐说，这番介绍看来纯属多余。

“咱们用不着介绍。”青年男子笑道，“你的事我听说过不少，德拉蒙德小姐。敝姓巴尼特。”

“‘弗拉什’·巴尼特。”塔莉娅说。他显得又吃惊，又不无得意。

“你听说过我，是么？”

“她什么没听说过。”麦克罗伊小姐一副无可奈何的口气，“再说，”她意味深长地补了一句，“她认得马尔，今晚还要同他吃饭呢。”

===Original===
Barnet looked sharply from one to the other, then back again at Milly Macroy.

“Have you told her anything?” he asked. There was a note of menace in his voice.

“You don’t have to tell her anything,” said Miss Macroy recklessly. “She knows it all!”

“Did you tell her?” he repeated.

“About Marl? No, I thought you’d tell her that.”

===Chinese===

巴尼特目光尖利，在两人之间来回一扫，末了又落回米莉·麦克罗伊身上。

“你跟她说了什么没有？”他问，话音里带着一丝威吓的调子。

“用不着跟她说什么。”麦克罗伊小姐豁出去似的说，“她全知道！”

“你到底说没说？”他又问了一遍。

“说马尔的事？没有，我寻思这该由你亲口告诉她。”

===Original===
The waiter brought the tea at that moment and there was a silence until he had gone.

“Now, I’m a plainspoken man,” said “Flush” Barnet. “And I’m going to tell you what I call you.”

“This sounds interesting,” said the girl, never taking her eyes from his face.

“I call you Thorough-Bad Thalia. How’s that? Good, eh?” said Mr. Barnet, leaning back in his chair and surveying her. “Thorough-Bad Thalia! You’re a naughty girl! I was in court the day old Froyant charged you with pinching!”

He shook his head waggishly.

===Chinese===

侍者恰在此时把茶端了上来，两人默然无声，直到他走开。

“我这人说话直。”“弗拉什”·巴尼特说，“我这就告诉你，我管你叫什么。”

“这倒有意思。”姑娘说，眼睛始终没离开他的脸。

“我管你叫纯种的坏种塔莉娅。怎么样，妙吧？”巴尼特先生说着，仰靠到椅背上，把她上下打量了一番，“纯种的坏种塔莉娅！你这个小坏丫头！老弗鲁瓦扬告你顺手牵羊那天，我就在法庭上！”

他一副促狭相，摇了摇头。

===Original===
“You’re as full of information as last year’s almanac,” said Thalia Drummond coolly. “I suppose you didn’t bring me here to exchange compliments?”

“No, I didn’t,” admitted “Flush” Barnet, and the jealous Miss Macroy recognised, by certain signs, the fascination that the girl was casting over her lover. “I brought you here to talk business. We’re all friends here, and we’re all in the same old business. I want to tell you straight away that I’m not one of your little thieving crooks, who lives from hand to mouth.”

He spoke very correctly, but aspirated his “h’s” just a trifle heavily Thalia duly remarked.

“I have people behind me who can find money to any amount if the job is good enough, and you’re spoiling a good pitch, Thalia.”

===Chinese===

“你肚子里的消息，跟去年的历书一样充实。”塔莉娅·德拉蒙德冷冷地说，“我猜，你请我来总不是为了彼此恭维吧？”

“不，不是。”“弗拉什”·巴尼特承认道；好吃醋的麦克罗伊小姐凭种种迹象，已看出这姑娘正在把她的情人迷得神魂颠倒。“我请你来是谈正事的。在座的都是朋友，干的也是同一行老营生。我先把话挑明，我可不是你们那种吃了上顿没下顿的小毛贼。”

他咬字吐音十分讲究，只是 h 这个音送得略重了些——这一点，塔莉娅当场便记下了。

“我背后有人，只要活儿够漂亮，要多少钱都能筹来。可你正在搅一块好场子，塔莉娅。”

===Original===
“Oh, I am, am I?” said Thalia. “Admitting I am all you think I am, in what way do I spoil the pitch?”

Mr. Barnet rolled his head from side to side with a smile.

“My dear girl,” he said with good-natured reproach. “How long do you think you’re going to last, taking money from envelopes and sending on old bits of paper? Eh? If my friend Brabazon hadn’t got the idea into his silly head that the fraud was worked in the post, you’d have had the police in your office in no time. And when I say my friend Brabazon, I’m not being funny, see?”

Here, he evidently thought he had said too much, though he found it very difficult indeed to leave the question of his friendship with the austere banker. Challenged, he might have said more, but Thalia offered no comment.

===Chinese===

“哦，我在搅，是吗？”塔莉娅说，“就算我全是你想的那种人，我又怎么搅了这好场子？”

巴尼特先生笑吟吟地把头从这边晃到那边。

“我亲爱的姑娘。”他好脾气地责备道，“从信封里抽钱、再把几张旧纸片转寄出去——你以为这营生还撑得了多久？嗯？要不是我那位朋友布拉巴宗犯糊涂，一门心思认定骗局出在邮寄环节上，警察早就进你的办公室了。我说‘我那位朋友布拉巴宗’，可不是打哈哈，明白吗？”

说到这里，他显然自觉失言，可要就此撇下他与那位古板银行家的交情这个话头，又实在难乎为继。倘或有人追问，他兴许还会多说几句，偏偏塔莉娅不置一词。

===Original===
“Now, I’m going to tell you something,” he leant over the table and regulated his voice. “Milly and me have been working Brabazon’s bank for two months. There’s a big lot of money to be got, but not out of the bank﻿—Brabazon is a friend of mine﻿—but it can be done through one of the clients, and the man with the biggest balance is Marl.”

Her lips curled for the second time that day.

“That’s where you’re wrong,” she said quietly. “Marl’s balance wouldn’t buy a row of beans.”

He stared at her incredulously, then looked at Milly Macroy with a frown.

“You told me that he had the best part of a hundred thousand﻿—”

===Chinese===

“好了，现在我要告诉你一件事。”他俯身向桌，把嗓音压得匀匀的。“我和米莉盯上布拉巴宗银行暗中做局，已经做了两个月。有大把的钞票好捞，但不是从银行里捞——布拉巴宗是我的朋友——这钱得从一位客户身上做出来，而存款最厚的那位，就是马尔。”

她的嘴角撇了下来——这已是当天第二回。

“这你可就说错了。”她轻声说，“马尔那点存款，买不来一把豆子。”

他难以置信地瞪着她，随即皱起眉头，看向米莉·麦克罗伊。

“你跟我说过，他少说也有十万镑——”

===Original===
“So he has,” said the girl.

“He had until today,” replied Thalia. “But this afternoon Mr. Brabazon went out﻿—I think he went to the Bank of England, because the notes were all new. He sent for me and I saw them stacked up on his desk. He told me he was closing Marl’s account, and that he was not the kind of man he wanted as a client. Then he took the money and called on Marl, I think, for when he came back just before the bank closed he handed me Marl’s cheque.”

“ ‘I’ve settled that account, Miss Drummond,’ he said. ‘I don’t think we’ll be troubled with that blackguard again.’ ”

“Did he know about Marl asking you out to dinner?” asked Milly, but the girl shook her head.

Mr. Barnet said nothing. He was sitting back in his chair, fondling his chin, with a faraway look in his eyes.

===Chinese===

“他是有的。”姑娘说。

“到今天为止是有。”塔莉娅答道，“可今天下午，布拉巴宗先生出去了——我想他是去了趟英格兰银行（Bank of England），因为那些钞票全是崭新的。他派人把我叫去，我亲眼看见钞票一沓沓码在他的桌上。他告诉我，他要结清马尔的账户，说马尔不是他想留的那种主顾。随后他带上那笔钱出门了，我想是亲自拜访马尔去了，因为银行关门前不久他回来，交给了我马尔开出的一张支票。”

“‘那笔账我已了结，德拉蒙德小姐，’他说，‘我想咱们不会再受那个恶棍的纠缠了。’”

“他知道马尔约你出去吃饭的事吗？”米莉问，姑娘却摇了摇头。

巴尼特先生一言不发。他仰靠在椅背上，摩挲着下巴，眼里一副悠悠出神的样子。

===Original===
“A big amount, was it?” he asked.

“Sixty-two thousand,” replied the girl.

“And it is in his house?” said Barnet, his face pink with excitement. “Sixty-two thousand! Did you hear that, Milly? And you’re dining with him tonight?” said “Flush” Barnet slowly and significantly. “Now, what about it?”

She met his gaze without flinching.

“What about what?” she asked.

===Chinese===

“数目不小吧？”他问。

“六万二千镑。”姑娘答道。

“而这会儿钱就在他家里？”巴尼特说，兴奋得满脸泛红，“六万二！米莉，你听见没有？今晚你就同他一道吃饭？”“弗拉什”·巴尼特慢条斯理、意味深长地说，“怎么样，干不干？”

她迎着他的目光，毫不动容。

“什么怎么样？”她问。

===Original===
“Here’s the chance of a lifetime,” he said, husky with emotion. “You’re going to the house. You’re not above stringing the old man along, are you, Thalia?”

She was silent.

“I know the place,” said “Flush” Barnet, “one of those quaint little houses in Kensington that cost a fortune to keep up. Marisburg Place, Bayswater Road.”

“I know the address pretty well,” said the girl.

“He keeps three menservants,” said “Flush” Barnet, “but they’re usually out any night he happens to be entertaining a lady friend. Do you get me?”

===Chinese===

“这是千载难逢的机会。”他说，激动得嗓音发哑，“你就要进那座宅子了。吊一吊那老头子的胃口，你总不至于不屑为之吧，塔莉娅？”

她默不作声。

“那地方我知道。”“弗拉什”·巴尼特说，“肯辛顿（Kensington）那种别致的小宅子，光是日常维持就要一大笔钱。贝斯沃特路的马里斯堡广场。”

“这地址我熟得很。”姑娘说。

“他养着三个男仆。”“弗拉什”·巴尼特说，“可逢上他招待女宾的夜晚，那几个照例都不在家。你听明白了吗？”

===Original===
“But he’s not entertaining me in his house,” said the girl.

“What’s the matter with a little bit of supper after the show, eh?” asked Barnet. “Suppose he puts it up to you, and you say yes. There’ll be no servants in the house when you get back. That I’ll take my oath. I’ve studied Marl.”

“What do you expect me to do? Rob him?” asked Thalia. “Stick a gun under his nose and say, ‘Deliver your pieces of eight’?”

“Don’t be a fool,” said Mr. Barnet, startled out of his pose of elegant gentleman. “You’re to do nothing but have your supper and come away. Keep him amused, make him laugh. You needn’t be frightened because I’ll be in the house soon after you, and if there’s any trouble I’ll be on hand.”

The girl was playing with her teaspoon, her eyes fixed on the tablecloth.

===Chinese===

“可他并不是要在自己宅子里招待我。”姑娘说。

“散了戏之后吃顿宵夜，这有什么打紧，嗯？”巴尼特问，“倘若他向你开口，你应了下来。等你们回到宅子，家里一个仆人也不会有。这话我可以起誓。马尔这个人，我研究透了。”

“你指望我做什么？抢他？”塔莉娅问，“拿枪顶住他的鼻子，说‘把你的八块银元交出来’？”

“别犯傻。”巴尼特先生说，那副优雅绅士的做派登时乱了套，“你只管吃你的宵夜，吃完走人。逗他开心，哄他发笑。你用不着害怕——你进宅子后不久我就到，万一出了岔子，我就在手边。”

姑娘摆弄着茶匙，两眼盯着桌布。

===Original===
“Suppose he doesn’t send his servants away?”

“You can bank on that,” interrupted Mr. Barnet. “Moses! There never was such a wonderful opportunity! Do you agree?”

Thalia shook her head.

“It is too big for me. Maybe you’re right and I’m likely to get into trouble, but it seems to me that petty pilfering is my long suit.”

“Bah!” said Barnet in disgust. “You’re mad! Now’s your time to make a harvest, my dear. You’re not known to the police. You’re not under the limelight like me. Are you going to do it?”

===Chinese===

“万一他不把仆人打发走呢？”

“这一点你尽管放心。”巴尼特先生插话道，“天哪！从没有过这么妙的机会！你答应不答应？”

塔莉娅摇了摇头。

“这票事对我而言太大了。也许你说得对，我迟早要惹祸上身，不过依我看，小偷小摸才是我的看家本事。”

“呸！”巴尼特一脸厌弃地说，“你疯了！亲爱的，眼下正是你大捞一票的时候。警方还不认得你这张脸。你也不像我，整天被探照灯照着。你到底干不干？”

===Original===
She dropped her eyes again to the cloth and again fidgeted with her spoon nervously.

“All right,” she said with a sudden shrug, “I might as well be hung for a sheep as a lamb.”

“Or for a good share of sixty thousand as for a miserable couple of hundred, eh?” said Barnet jovially, and beckoned the waiter.

===Chinese===

她重又垂下眼帘盯着桌布，紧张地把小勺拨来拨去。

“好吧。”她忽地耸了耸肩，“反正偷羊羔是上绞架，偷大羊也是上绞架。”

“与其为可怜巴巴的一两百镑上绞架，不如为六万镑里的一大份上绞架，是吧？”巴尼特乐呵呵地说，一面招手唤侍者。

===Original===
Thalia left the restaurant and turned homeward. She had to pass the bank, and it was not good policy, she thought, to hail a taxicab until she had left the neighbourhood, where Mr. Brabazon’s grave eyes might observe her extravagance. She had turned into the stream of pedestrians that thronged Regent Street at this hour when she felt a touch on her arm, and turned.

A young man was walking by her side, a good-looking, keen-faced young man who did not smile ingratiatingly as others had done who had nudged her arm in Regent Street, nor did he inquire if she were going the same way as he.

“Thalia!”

She turned quickly at the sound of the voice, and for a second her self-possession failed her.

===Chinese===

塔莉娅出了餐馆，转身往家走。路上总要经过那家银行，她想，还没走出这一带就扬手叫出租汽车，实在不是上策——布拉巴宗先生那双严峻的眼睛，说不定正瞧着她这番铺张。她正汇入此时挤满摄政街（Regent Street）的人流，忽觉有人碰了碰她的手臂，便回过头去。

一个青年男子走在她身旁，眉目英俊，面庞精明。他不曾像摄政街上那些用胳膊肘碰过她的男人那样讨好地赔笑，也没有问她是否与他同路。

“塔莉娅！”

听见这声音，她霍然回头，一时间竟失了镇定。

===Original===
“Mr. Beardmore!” she faltered.

Jack’s face was flushed and he was obviously embarrassed.

“I only wanted to speak to you for a moment. I have waited for a week for the opportunity,” he said hurriedly.

“You knew I was at Brabazon’s﻿—who told you?”

He hesitated.

===Chinese===

“比德莫尔先生！”她讷讷地说。

杰克满脸涨红，显然十分尴尬。

“我只想同你说几句话。为这个机会，我已经等了一个星期。”他急匆匆地说。

“你知道我在布拉巴宗银行——是谁告诉你的？”

他迟疑了一下。

===Original===
“Inspector Parr,” he said, and when he saw the smile curl on the girl’s lips, he went on: “Old Parr isn’t a bad sort, really. He has never said another word against you, Thalia.”

“Another!” she quoted, “but does it really matter? And now, Mr. Beardmore, I really must go. I have a very important engagement.”

But he held fast to her hand.

“Thalia, won’t you tell me why you did it?” he asked quietly. “Who is behind you?”

She laughed.

===Chinese===

“帕尔探长。”他说。见姑娘唇边浮起一抹讥笑，他接着说：“老帕尔人不坏，真的。他再没说过你一句坏话，塔莉娅。”

“‘再’！”她拾着他这个字眼，“可这当真要紧吗？好了，比德莫尔先生，我实在得走了。我有个非常要紧的约会。”

可他紧紧攥着她的手不放。

“塔莉娅，你难道不肯告诉我，你为什么要那样做？”他轻声问，“你背后是谁？”

她笑了。

===Original===
“There is a reason for your keeping this extraordinary company,” he went on, when she stopped him.

“What extraordinary company?” she demanded.

“You have just come from a restaurant,” he said. “You have been there with a man called ‘Flush’ Barnet, a notorious crook and a man who has served a term of penal servitude. The woman with you was Milly Macroy, a confederate of his who was concerned in the Darlington Cooperative robbery and has also served a term of imprisonment. At present she is engaged at Brabazon’s Bank.”

“Well?” said the girl again.

“Surely you don’t know the character of these people?” urged Jack.

===Chinese===

“你结交这路非同寻常的人物，必是有缘故的。”他还想说下去，却被她拦住了。

“什么非同寻常的人物？”她诘问道。

“你方才从一家餐馆出来。”他说，“同你在一起的那个男人，名叫‘弗拉什’·巴尼特，是个声名狼藉的骗子，服过一期苦役。同你在一起的那个女人是米莉·麦克罗伊，他的同伙，牵涉进达灵顿合作社（Darlington Cooperative）劫案，也坐过牢。眼下她在布拉巴宗银行当差。”

“嗯？”姑娘又应了一声。

“你总不至于不知道这些人的底细吧？”杰克恳切地说。

===Original===
“And how do you know them?” she asked calmly. “Am I wrong in supposing that you were not alone in your﻿—vigil? Were you accompanied by the admirable Mr. Parr? I see you were. Why, you are almost a policeman yourself, Mr. Beardmore.”

Jack was staggered.

“Do you realise that it is Parr’s duty to inform your employer that you keep that kind of company?” he asked. “For heaven’s sake, Thalia, take a sane view of your position.”

But she laughed.

“Heaven forbid that I should interfere with the duty of a responsible police officer,” she said, “but on the whole I’d rather Mr. Parr didn’t. That at least is a sign of grace,” she smiled. “Yes, I’d much rather he didn’t. I don’t mind the police speaking to me for my good because it is only right and proper that they should try to lead the weak from their sinful ways. But an employer who attempts to reform an erring girl might be a bit of a nuisance, don’t you think?”

===Chinese===

“那你怎么会知道他们的底细？”她心平气和地问，“我若猜你那回——守候——并非独自一人，总不见得猜错吧？陪着你的是不是那位可敬的帕尔先生？我看得出来，正是。哎呀，你自己都快算得上一个警察了，比德莫尔先生。”

杰克被惊得说不出话来。

“你可意识到，把你的雇主知会一声、说你结交这路朋友，本是帕尔的职责？”他问，“千万看在老天的份上，塔莉娅，对自己的处境清醒一点吧。”

可她笑了。

“上天也不容我妨碍一位尽职警官的公务。”她说，“不过总的来说，我还是宁可帕尔先生别去多这个嘴。这至少算是人家留了情面。”她莞尔一笑，“对，我巴不得他别去。警察为我的好处来开导我，我不介意——设法把软弱的人从作孽的歧途上领回来，本是他们名正言顺的分内事。可是一位一心想感化失足姑娘的雇主，就未免有点招人嫌了，你说是不是？”

===Original===
In spite of himself he laughed.

“Really, Thalia, you’re much too clever for the kind of company you’re keeping and for the kind of life you’re drifting to,” he added earnestly. “I know I have no right to interfere, but perhaps I could help you. Particularly,” he hesitated, “if you have done something which places you in the power of these people.”

She put out her hand with a rare smile.

“Goodbye,” she said sweetly, and left him feeling something of a fool.

===Chinese===

他不由得笑了。

“说真的，塔莉娅，凭你这份聪明，去结交这路人、滑向那种生活，实在不值。”他恳切地又说，“我知道我无权干涉，可没准儿我能帮上你。尤其是——”他迟疑了一下——“倘若你已经做了什么事，叫自己落进了这些人的手心。”

她伸出手来，脸上绽出一抹难得的微笑。

“再见。”她甜甜地说了这一句，丢下他自个儿站着，自觉活像个傻子。

===Original===
The girl walked quickly through Burlington Arcade to Piccadilly and entered a taxi. The block of mansions at which she alighted was situated in the Marylebone Road and was a distinct improvement on Lexington Street.

The liveried porter took her up in the elevator to the third floor, and she let herself into a flat which was both prettily and expensively furnished.

She pressed a bell, and it was answered by a staid middle-aged woman.

“Martha,” she said, “I shan’t want any tea, thank you. Lay out my blue evening gown and telephone to Waltham’s Garage and tell them that I shall want a car to be here at five minutes before half past seven.”

Miss Drummond’s wages from the bank were exactly £4 a week.

===Chinese===

姑娘快步穿过伯灵顿拱廊（Burlington Arcade），走到皮卡迪利（Piccadilly），上了一辆出租汽车。她下车的那片高级公寓大楼坐落在玛丽勒本路上，比起莱克星顿街的旧居，不啻高出一等。

穿制服的门房送她乘电梯上到四楼，她用自己的钥匙开门进了一套公寓，屋里陈设得既雅致又阔气。

她按了按铃，应门的是一位稳重的中年妇人。

“玛莎（Martha），”她说，“茶就不用了，谢谢。把我那件蓝色晚礼服取出来，再给沃尔瑟姆车行（Waltham’s Garage）挂个电话，告诉他们七点二十五分我要用车。”

德拉蒙德小姐在银行领的薪水，每周不多不少，整整四镑。

## XVI / 十六

===Original===
Mr. Marl Goes Out

===Chinese===
马尔先生一去不返

===Original===
“So you’ve come, eh?” said Mr. Marl, rising to greet the girl. “My word, but you look smart! And you look lovely, my dear, too!”

He took both her hands in his and led her into the little gold and white drawing-room.

“Lovely!” he repeated in an almost hushed voice. “I can tell you I was a little bit scared about taking you to the Ritz-Carlton. You don’t mind my frankness, do you﻿—have a cigarette?”

He fumbled in the tail-pocket of his dress coat, produced a large gold case and opened it.

“You thought I’d turn up in one of Morne & Gillingsworth’s six guinea models, eh?” she laughed as she lit the cigarette.

===Chinese===
“这么说你来了，嗯？”马尔先生起身迎接那姑娘，“哎呀，你打扮得真俏！亲爱的小姐，你人也生得可爱极了！”

他双手握住她的两只手，领她走进那间金白两色的小客厅。

“可爱！”他几乎压着嗓子又赞叹了一遍，“不瞒你说，带你上丽思卡尔顿大饭店（Ritz-Carlton），我心里还真有点发怵呢。你不介意我说话直吧？——来支烟？”

他在燕尾服后摆的口袋里摸索了一阵，掏出一只大金烟盒，打开来。

“你原以为我会穿着莫恩-吉林斯沃思（Morne & Gillingsworth）家六几尼一件的现成货色露面吧，嗯？”她点着烟，笑道。

===Original===
“Well, I did, my dear. I’ve had a lot of unhappy experiences,” explained Marl as he seated himself heavily in an armchair. “I’ve had ’em turn up in queer clothes, I can tell you!”

“Do you make a practice of entertaining the young and the fair?” Thalia had seated herself on the big padded fireguard and was looking down at him under her half-closed lids.

“Well,” said Mr. Marl complacently, rubbing his hands. “I’m not so old that I don’t get some pleasure out of ladies’ society. But you’re stunning!”

He was a blonde, red-faced man with suspiciously brown hair, suspiciously even teeth, and for this evening he had acquired a waist which seemed wholly unreal.

===Chinese===
“这个嘛，我还真这样想过，亲爱的。我有过不少不痛快的经历。”马尔一边解释，一边沉甸甸地落进扶手椅里，“不瞒你说，她们穿得怪里怪气就跑来的事，我见得多了！”

“款待年轻貌美的姑娘，是你的家常便饭么？”塔莉娅已在包着衬垫的大炉挡上坐定，半阖着眼帘居高临下地望着他。

“呃，”马尔先生搓着手，颇为自得，“我还没老到从女伴身上讨不着乐趣的地步。不过你可真是美得惊人！”

他是个金发红脸的汉子，头发棕得可疑，牙齿齐得可疑；而且为了今晚，他还添了一副怎么看都不像真的腰身。

===Original===
“We’re going to dinner and then we’ll go on and see ‘The Boys and the Girls’ at the Winter Palace,” he said, “and then,” he hesitated, “what do you say to a little supper?” he asked.

“A little supper? I don’t take supper,” said the girl.

“Well, you can peck a bit of fruit, I suppose?” suggested Mr. Marl.

“Where?” asked the girl steadily. “Most of the restaurants are closed before the theatres are out, aren’t they?”

===Chinese===
“我们先去吃晚饭，然后接着到冬宫剧院（Winter Palace）看《小伙子与姑娘们》，”他说，“再然后么——”他迟疑了一下，“来一顿小小的夜宵如何？”他问。

“一顿夜宵？我是不吃夜宵的。”姑娘说。

“那么，多少啄两口水果总可以吧？”马尔先生提议道。

“上哪儿去吃？”姑娘不动声色地问，“大多数馆子在散戏之前就打烊了，不是么？”

===Original===
“There’s no reason why you shouldn’t come back here? You’re not a prude, my dear, are you?”

“Not much,” she confessed.

“I can see you home in my car,” he said.

“I’ve got my own car, thank you,” said the girl, and Mr. Marl’s eyes opened. Then he began to laugh steadily at first, and his laughter ended in an asthmatical paroxysm. Presently he gasped: “Oh, you wicked little devil!”

===Chinese===
“那你还有什么不能回这儿来的道理呢？你总不是个假正经吧，亲爱的？”

“倒不至于。”她坦承道。

“我可以用我的车送你回家。”他说。

“我有自己的车，多谢。”姑娘说。马尔先生的眼睛睁圆了。起初他还沉稳地笑，笑着笑着便成了一阵哮喘似的发作，末了喘着气道：“嗬，你这个小妖精！”

---

===Original===
The evening was an interesting one for Thalia, more interesting by reason of the fact that she caught a glimpse of Mr. “Flush” Barnet in the hall of the hotel as she passed through.

It was after the theatre was over and they were standing in the vestibule, waiting for the lift-man to call their car, that Thalia showed some symptom of hesitation, but the eloquent Mr. Felix Marl overcame whatever reluctance she felt, and as the clock was striking the half hour after eleven she passed into the hall, not failing to notice that Mr. Marl did not ring for his servants, but let himself in with his own latchkey.

The supper was laid in a rose-panelled dining-room.

“I will help you, my dear,” said Mr. Marl. “We won’t bother about the servants.” But she shook her head.

===Chinese===
对塔莉娅来说，这个晚上很有意思；更有意思的是，她穿过饭店大厅时，瞥见了“弗拉什”·巴尼特先生的身影。

戏散场之后，两人站在前厅里，等开电梯的仆役去叫车，塔莉娅显出了几分迟疑，但能言善道的菲利克斯·马尔先生把她那点勉强悉数化解；时钟敲响十一点半时，她走进了宅中的门厅，而且没有忽略一点：马尔先生没有按铃唤仆人，而是用自备的弹簧钥匙自己开的门。

夜宵摆在餐厅里，那间屋子的护墙板镶着玫瑰花纹。

“我亲自来伺候你，亲爱的，”马尔先生说，“就不麻烦仆人们了。”可她摇了摇头。

===Original===
“I can eat nothing, and I think I’ll go home now,” she said.

“Wait, wait,” he begged. “I want to have a little talk with you about your boss. I can do you a lot of good in that firm﻿—at the bank, Thalia. Who called you Thalia?”

“My godfathers and godmothers, M. or N.,” said Thalia solemnly, and Mr. Marl squeaked his delight at her humour.

He was passing behind her, ostensibly to reach one of the dishes which were set on the table, when he stooped and, had she not slipped from his grasp, would have kissed her.

===Chinese===
“我什么都吃不下，我想我现在就回家。”她说。

“别急，别急，”他央求道，“我想跟你稍稍谈谈你那位东家。在那家公司里，我能给你帮大忙——在那家银行里，塔莉娅。是谁给你取的‘塔莉娅’这个名字？”

“我的教父和教母，‘某甲或某乙’。”塔莉娅一本正经地答道，马尔先生让她这份风趣逗得吱吱直乐。

他从她身后绕过，说是去够桌上摆着的某一盘菜，却弯下腰来；若不是她从他掌握中滑脱，这一吻早已落下了。

===Original===
“I think I’ll go home,” said Thalia.

“Rubbish!” Mr. Marl was annoyed, and when Mr. Marl was annoyed he forgot that he made any pretensions to gentle birth. “Come and sit down.”

She looked at him long and thoughtfully, and then, turning suddenly, went to the door, and turned the handle. It was locked.

“I think you had better open this door, Mr. Marl,” she said quietly.

===Chinese===
“我想我该回家了。”塔莉娅说。

“胡说！”马尔先生恼了；马尔先生一恼，就忘了他素来自诩的什么高贵出身。“过来坐下。”

她久久地、若有所思地望着他，随后猛一转身，走到门边拧动把手。门锁上了。

“我看你最好把这扇门打开，马尔先生。”她平静地说。

===Original===
“I think not,” chuckled Mr. Marl. “Now, Thalia, be the dear, good little girl I thought you were.”

“I should hate to dissipate any illusions you may have about my character,” said Thalia coolly. “You’ll open that door, please.”

“Certainly.”

He ambled toward the door, feeling in his pocket, then before she could realise his intention he had seized her in his arms. He was a powerful man, a head taller than she, and his big hands gripped her arms like steel clamps.

===Chinese===
“我看不必。”马尔先生咯咯直笑，“好了，塔莉娅，做回我原先以为的那个乖巧可人的小姑娘吧。”

“你对我的人品只怕存着些美妙的幻想，我真不忍叫它们破灭。”塔莉娅冷冷地说，“劳驾，把门打开。”

“当然。”

他慢悠悠朝门口踱去，手在口袋里摸索着，随即趁她还没明白过来他的意图，一把将她搂进怀里。他是个有力气的男人，比她高出一头，两只大手像钢钳似的攥住她的胳膊。

===Original===
“Let me go,” said Thalia steadily. She did not lose her nerve nor show the least sign of fear.

Suddenly he felt her tense muscles relax. He had conquered.

With a quick intake of breath he released his hold of the sullen girl.

“Let me have some supper,” she said, and he beamed.

===Chinese===
“放开我。”塔莉娅镇定地说。她既没乱了方寸，也没露出一丝惧色。

突然，他觉得她紧绷的肌肉松了下来。他得逞了。

他猛吸一口气，松开了那个绷着脸的姑娘。

“给我来点夜宵吧。”她说。他登时喜笑颜开。

===Original===
“Now, my dear, you are being the little girl I﻿—what’s that?”

The last was a squeak of terror.

She had strolled slowly to the table and had taken up the brocade bag. He had watched her and thought she was seeking a handkerchief.
